i [SOURCE: Page_5]

English abstract To meet the challenges of the scarcity of good quality water resources, the reuse of treated wastewater ("REUT" in French or "REUSE" in English) appears to be a solution for the future. It prevents the treated water from being released into the environment without giv- ing it a second life, or even more. To do this, we are promoting a paradigm shift in which wastewater is no longer seen as waste to be depolluted but a new resource that can be treated on a custom basis, depending on the needs and uses in new energy recovery facilities, to recover nutrients and other molecules of interest. This sustainable management of water resources comes up against many challenges, in particular technical problems such as the lack of flexibility of current wastewater treatment plants. Faced with the great challenge of providing, in a dynamic and economically acceptable manner, a quality of treated wa- ter adapted to the needs of agriculture, and in order to reduce the environmental impacts, automatic control technologies certainly have an essential role to play. This study, which is freed from current regulatory limitations as it is essentially carried out in simulations, presents the preliminary results of research with the development of a simulation platform for a flexible system combining activated sludge treatment plants with a membrane filtra- tion process. We evaluate how the control of the whole, based on the use of industrial controllers and advanced control, can be used to adapt in real time, under conditions of martial measurements and in the presence of uncertainties and disturbances, a water whose the quality meets the needs of the plants / crops or, on the contrary, when necessary, meets the quality constraints required for release into the environment. The approach requires knowing the needs of plants. As the latter are difficult to continuously measure, part of the work is dedicated to the synthesis of unknown input observers to estimate the amount of water available for the plant in the soil (and therefore accessible for evapotranspiration). Finally, since the cost of wastewater treated by membrane filtration technologies is often prohibitive for users, we are evaluating the extent to which recourse to optimal control approaches, in particular the Maximum Pontryagin Principle, would make it possible to reduce these cost by optimizing the management of the clogging of these membranes and thus minimizing the functioning cost of these systems. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Keywords: Flexibility, Modelling, Control, Reuse, Optimization, Wastewater treatment, Observation. [SOURCE: Page_5]

iii [SOURCE: Page_7]

Résumé en Français Pour répondre aux enjeux de la raréfaction des ressources en eau de bonne qualité, la réutilisation des eaux usées traitées (‘REUT’ ou ‘REUSE’) apparaît comme une solution d’avenir. Elle permet de ne pas rejeter l’eau traitée dans l’environnement sans lui faire subir une seconde vie, voire davantage. Pour cela, nous promouvons un changement de paradigme dans lequel les eaux usées ne sont plus vues comme des déchets à dépolluer mais une nouvelle ressource pouvant être traitée à façon, dépendant des besoins et des usages dans de nouvelles installations de récupération d’énergie, de nutriments et autres molécules d’intérêt. Cette gestion de la ressource en eau se heurte à de nombreux défis, no- tamment des problèmes techniques tels que le manque de flexibilité des stations d’épuration actuelles. Face au grand défi de fournir, de manière dynamique et économiquement accept- able, une qualité d’eau traitée adaptée aux besoins de l’agriculture, et afin de réduire les impacts environnementaux, les technologies de l’automatique ont assurément un rôle es- sentiel à jouer. Cette étude, qui s’affranchit des limitations règlementaires actuelles car essentiellement menée en simulations, présente les résultats préliminaires de recherches combinant le développement d’une plateforme de simulation d’un système flexible combi- nant des systèmes de traitement à boues activées à un procédé de filtration membranaire. Nous évaluons comment le contrôle de l’ensemble, fondé sur l’utilisation de controleurs industriels et de commande avancée, peut être utilisé pour adapter en temps réel, en con- ditions de mesures partielles et en présence d’incertitudes et de perturbations, une eau dont la qualité répond aux besoins des plantes/cultures ou, au contraire, lorsque néces- saire, est conforme aux exigences de qualité requises pour un rejet dans l’environnement. L’approche nécessite la connaissance des besoins des plantes. Ces dernières étant difficile- ment mesurables en continu, une partie du travail est dédiée à la synthèse d’observateurs à entrées inconnues pour estimer la quantité d’eau disponible pour la plante dans les sols (et donc accessible pour l’évapotranspiration). Enfin, le coût de l’eau usées traitée par les technologies de filtration membranaire étant souvent rédhibitoire pour les irrigants, nous évaluons dans quelle mesure le recours à des approches de contrôle optimal, notamment le Principe du Maximum de Pontryagin, permettrait de faire baisser ces coût en optimisant la gestion du colmatage de ces membranes et donc le coût de fonctionnement de ces sys- tèmes. [SOURCE: Page_7]

Mots clés: Flexibilité, Modélisation, Contrôle, Réutilisation, Optimisation, Traitement des eaux usées, Observation. [SOURCE: Page_7]

Acknowledgments - Remerciements This PhD thesis is a result of three years of work and a good souvenir that I will always keep in my mind. To all of you that have contributed to this work: thank you! Without you, this would never have been possible (even if there is not enough space to list you all, be sure that you have a special place in my heart). [SOURCE: Page_9]

Firstly, I would like to express my gratitude to the members of the reviewing com- mittee: Mrs. Isabelle Queinnec, Mr. Ibrahim Cherki, Mr. Marc Héran, Mrs. Amal Choukchou, Mrs. Sihem Tebbani, Mr. Karim Yadi, Mrs.Samia Semcheddine and Mr. Claude Lobry, for generously offering their time, support, guidance and good will throughout the preparation and review of this work, that significantly improve the quality of this PhD thesis. [SOURCE: Page_9]

Un merci à mes encadrants de thèse : Jérôme Harmand et Boumediéne Benyahia. Cela a été un énorme plaisir de travailler avec vous. Merci de m’avoir guidé et de m’avoir aidé à tisser les liens entre 4 sujets extrêmement riches : la modélisation, le contrôle, l’estimation et l’optimisation des procédés! Je vous remercie aussi énormément pour toutes les discussions pendant ces trois années de thèse et principalement de m’avoir donné l’exemple de l’écoute avec un énorme respect de l’autre. [SOURCE: Page_9]

J’adresse mes vifs remerciements à mon directeur de thèse, Jérome Harmand. Pour la premiere fois excusez-moi de TUTOYER!, Ton encadrement a tout simplement été exemplaire. Merci pour toute l’aide qui tu m’as apporté durant cette thèse. je ne peux que te remercier de m’avoir permis, à la fois, de m’éloigner de mon sujet « classique » vers une curiosité scientifique. Merci pour la confiance qui tu m’as donné pour réaliser ce projet de thèse innovant. Merci à ton esprit collaboratif qui m’a permis d’être un membre dans l’un des grands projet européen ‘Control^4Reuse’. Merci pour ta patience, ton écoute et tes encouragements durant les moments difficiles, malgré ton emploi du temps chargé, merci de me supporter lors des moments du grand stress qui reflètent tes bonnes qualités humaine. Merci pour ton expertise, tes corrections et tes conseils qui m’ont permis d’améliorer mes recherches. J’ai beaucoup appris de toi et heureux de voir le fruit du ce travail immense. [SOURCE: Page_9]

Je tiens à exprimer ma gratitude à mon co-encadrant de thèse Boumediene Benyahia pour ses conseils, sa collaboration et son aide toujours précieuse. Merci pour vos aides et vos orientations qui m’ont permis d’arriver à cette étape, vous étiez toujours comme un frére qui m’avez soutenu, et qui a cru en moi dans les moments difficiles. je vous remercie éneormemnt. [SOURCE: Page_9]

Je tiens à remercier Nicolas Bernet le directeur de LBE-INRAE. Merci à Jean Philippe Steyer pour ses orientations et ses conseils. Un merci particulier pour Sylvie, Nadine et Alexandra qui sont toujours là pour nous aider. Merci pour votre disponibilité et votre expertise qui apporte beaucoup au LBE. Merci à Jérôme Hamelin pour l’aide et les [SOURCE: Page_9]

vi [SOURCE: Page_10]

conseils qu’il m’a porté pendant les comités de suivi de thése. Merci à Eric Trably pour ses encouragements. Merci à toutes et à tous qui j’ai rencontré au LBE, Merci à Rémi, Dominique, Renaud, Elie, Diana, Kim, Julie, Eric Latrille, Virginie et tous les personnels [SOURCE: Page_10]

Je voudrais aussi remercie toutes et tous mes collègues que j’ai rencontré pendant ces années passées au LBE. Then, a particular thanks is addressed to Alain Rapaport for sharing with me his ex- pertise in the field of optimal control and for your guidance during the whole duration of my theses and during the thesis committees. Thank you very much Mr. Denis Douchain for learning me innovative control strategies, particularly cascade control and for your availability to answer my questions either remotely or during my stay at UCLouvain, Belgium, ICTEAM. I would also like to thank you for accepting me in your laboratory and for lending me your office and some essential coffee capsules. Many thanks also to all the administrative staff of ICTEAM, in particular to Marie-Christine Joveneau. [SOURCE: Page_10]

I would like to thank Prof. Marc Héran for sharing with me the interest about the development of the innovative flexible platform, that used in this work, thank you also for accepting to be the referent of my work thesis and for all your guidance which you gave me all along this thesis. [SOURCE: Page_10]

I would like to express all my feelings, my profound respect and my sincere grati- tudes to Brahim CHERKI, professor at the University of Tlemcen. You are for me an example of an ideal professor, your qualities, both human and professional, They reflect your good spirit and your kind intention [SOURCE: Page_10]

My thoughts naturally go to the Control^4Reuse project members , thanks for the Swedish colleagues: Eva Thorin, Monica Odlare, Erik Dahlquist, Heidi Marais and Eva Nordlander, and thanks for the Brazilian colleagues : Michela Mulas, Francesco Corona and Minho Otacilio, for all the exchanges on Modeling and control of WWTP and especially for the time dedicated for sharing with me their expertise to improve the quality of this PhD thesis. [SOURCE: Page_10]

My last words are addressed to my familly, Thank you Mummy Dearest for your wishes for me, I know you would be so proud of me. Thank you Dad for always supporting me in my decisions, even my most difficult ones. You are my real teachers and the reason I am who I am today. Thank you also to my brothers and sisters. 7 [SOURCE: Page_24]

General Introduction I. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_24]

World population growth, climate change, decline in biodiversity and increase of food demand are creating a dual challenge for both emerging and developed countries in terms of environmental protection and water demand. At a global scale, wastewater treatment (realized in wastewater treatment plants or WWTP) is one of the essential aspects of water resource preservation, notably since, whatever its quality, wastewater is always finally released in the environment. Water scarcity - or water stress - is a phenomenon that occurs when the demand for water exceeds the available resources. In 2025, more than half of the world’s population will live in water-stressed regions [OMS ]. The United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) estimates that water resources are sufficient if the amount available per person per year is greater than or equal to 1700 m^3 . Less than 1000 m^3 per person per year corresponds to a water stress situation [PNU ]. Water stress can have important consequences for the environment, economy, public health, etc..., and it may lead to important conflicts of use (e.g. agriculture vs. food demand). About 70% of total water consumption worldwide is used for agriculture. In 2017, 4000 Km$_3$ of water were withdrawn over the world: on this volume only 1,2% is reclaimed after treatment and 6,3% is directly reused without treatment. This situation leads to a worrisome imbalance between demand and availability of water resources. Even if the water resource is saved, which is an obligation in view of the looming shortage, the Agriculture will continue to develop in the world: agricultural production was multiplied by a factor of between 2.5 and 3 over the last 50 years but the cultivated area increased by only 12%. More than 40 % of the increase in food production was provided by irrigated areas, which doubled in surface. During the same period, the area of cultivated land per person has gradually decreased to less than 0.25 ha Fig. 1. Clear evidence of shortage of water sources for irrigation in the regions with water scarcity appears all around the world. In some regions, the overall increase in production has been associated with the degradation of soil and water resources and the worsening of the goods and services provided by the ecosystems involved as biomass, carbon fixation, soil quality, biodiversity, water retention and supply. The increasing water scarcity requires developing efficient technologies that would allow the use of treated wastewater as an ‘unconventional water resource’, by treating them to a level of quality adapted to the user’s needs and usages. [SOURCE: Page_24]

The reasons leading to water stress and the necessity to protect the environment are relevant motivations to develop the reuse of treated wastewater and protect water re- sources. From an environmental point of view, the reuse of treated wastewater has many advantages. In the case of sensitive natural areas, the benefit is to reduce the volumes rejected into the receiving environment and better control it while limiting biodiversity decline. In addition, the exploitation of this resource makes it possible to reduce the demand on surface freshwater bodies, which often have a high environmental value (wetlands for example). This pressure reduction is achieved either indirectly by using [SOURCE: Page_24]

8 [SOURCE: Page_25]

Figure 1: Evolution of irrigated and non-irrigated cultivated areas (1961-2008) FAO(2010b) [SOURCE: Page_25]

treated wastewater instead of another resource, or directly by the transfer of good quality water where needed. In the latter case, recharging or maintaining the quality of water reserves (surface or underground) for drinking water is often also an issue. Treated wastewater is an economically interesting resource, because it is constant in quantity and, if its quality is controlled, it opens the route to a better management during water scarcity periods to maintain stable agricultural and industrial production. Although, when treated wastewater is reused in agriculture, it does not recharge rivers, this can cause major problems for closed systems such as the Mediterranean Sea, especially since it has a large number of desalination plants, but the reuse of treated wastewater in agriculture remains an alternative that contributes, when relevant, to reduce the pressure on good quality resources. However, the reuse of treated wastewater is still relatively in its infancy and limited throughout the world, due to many constraints that impede the advancement of this field. Some of these impediments are environmental, notably related to salinization risks and to the possible diffusion of heavy metals and micropollutants (cf. in Section. Health risks in reuse chain), while others are sanitary (possible dissemination of pathogens or antibiotic resistance gene - ARG) and economic, such as the ability to guarantee the health of users and to make the reuse of treated wastewater in agriculture acceptable by the citizens. While it is not allowed by the regulation to keep too much nutrients in effluent, the aim of this PhD thesis is to investigate how the use of modelling and control techniques could be used to optimize treatment plants for water reuse and in particular how the quality of treated wastewater could be adapted to crops needs. Indeed, the demand over time of the agriculture is not the same as the ‘logic of availability’: the ‘production’ of treated wastewater is almost regular throughout the year, while the demand for water from crops is modulated by agricultural cycles and seasons. It will then be necessary either, to store the treated wastewater ‘produced’ during periods when there is no demand for water (in which [SOURCE: Page_25]

9 [SOURCE: Page_26]

case, the quantity required by crops or plants may correspond to the available quantity of ‘stored’ treated wastewater, but not the quality), or create a system allowing to operate the WWTP in a flexible way in order to adapt both quantity and quality of wastewater treated ‘produced’ to the crops needs to come up with the notion of WRRF. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Within the framework of this PhD thesis, the ‘flexibility’ of a process refers to the ability of a treatment system to be controlled dynamically in such a way the quality of the treated water can be adapted to the quality required for its effective reuse. Thus, the reuse of treated wastewater requires a transition between two operation modes, called hereafter degraded- or full- modes. [SOURCE: Page_26]

The concept of ‘adapting treatment to the needs of plants/crops’ in a flexible treat- ment system may be optimized using closed-loop operations, where the water and nutrient needs must be continuously provided to the flexible treatment system (the WRRF) as a set point, either by measurements on the plants/crops themselves, or using mathematical models. Solving optimal control problems subject to disturbances (weather, input effluent characteristics) allow to generate such setpoints in terms of water contents (nutrients) and quantity to be delivered to the plants. There are many objectives that may be pursued with respect to the optimal control problems : they can be the maximization of the biomass produced during a season, the minimization of plant water needs or still the minimization of nutrients delivered to the plants under production yields constraints. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Within this thesis, most of the associated risks are neglected or managed through technological considerations. One of the main risks - the sanitary risk related to the dissemination of pathogens into the environment - is supposed to be negligible as long as membrane bioreactors - through membrane filtration - are considered. The membranes are increasingly used as advanced treatment systems, by the combination of a biological treatment using activated sludge and liquid/solid separation by membrane. Using such systems allows one to achieve a water quality that is well adapted to reuse in ensuring a high retention degree of suspended solids. But the main drawback is the fouling of the membranes. It is the main reason for which this process is associated to higher operating energy costs than conventional activated sludge processes where a settling tank is used. This reduces the competitivity of MBR. The development of high-performance membrane reactors in optimizing filtration cleaning over the long term is therefore a major challenge. As membrane fouling is a complex mechanism, it remains difficult to control, despite the implementation of cleaning strategies such as chemical washing, injection of gas bubbles in the vicinity of the membrane, relaxation or backwashing [Charfi et al. 2015]. The control of membrane fouling could reduce the costs associated with membrane maintenance. The development of an optimal strategy for reducing energy consumption costs allows to promote the use of membranes in advanced treatment systems intended for reuse, to provide high quality treated water on the one hand, and to improve the performance of the flexible system for resource recovery on the other hand. [SOURCE: Page_26]

The present studies represent the first steps towards the development of a flexible [SOURCE: Page_26]

10 [SOURCE: Page_27]

system of a thoroughly designed and controlled complete reclamation chain for the treatment and reuse of treated wastewater for agricultural purposes. This aims at show- casing the advancements within the notion of wastewater treatment plant flexibility using modelling and control tools and stimulating the interaction with the automatic control at large. [SOURCE: Page_27]

II. How improving reuse of treated wastewater? The current wastewater treatment plants (represented in the first figure of Fig. 2) aims to treat wastewater at a degree such as normative constraints are verified for the water to be released into the environment while the resulting sludge is, at best, used as fertilizer for agricultural and industrial needs. Such processes are not designed to carry out a treatment aimed at adapting the quality of the treated wastewater to the uses. It is based on the injection of an important amount of energy and produces CO$_2$ and possibly nitrogen oxide, a powerful greenhouse gas. [SOURCE: Page_27]

The perspective of this system needs to be changed to reflect the paradigm shift to- wards a resource recovery approach as promoted by circular economy [Sfez et al. 2019]. In such a ‘vision for the future’, much less energy is injected into the system through energy recovery while a ‘flexible system’ may be optimized in such a way to precisely produce energy and water characteristics adapted to (possibly new) usages (cf. second figure of Fig. 2). [SOURCE: Page_27]

In an ideal situation (cf. the third figure of Fig. 2), the control diagram involves an ‘integrated system’ composed of the entire treatment chain - from the resource to the soil and plants - the input of this system being composed of the wastewater input characteristics while the output is the production and the state of the biomass of interest (or the state of the different usages we can think of). In this case, the challenge is to design a decision support/control system able to optimally compute the control inputs from the available measurements under a number of practical constraints and external disturbances. To do so, it is necessary to couple models of different natures that are models of WWTP/WRRF, models of water transport/storage, models of soil and plants... Such coupling is very difficult and some models, like models of plants are not so mature than those used for the biological treatment of wastewater. It is why, instead of considering this ideal vision, we will describe in the next section a slighly different approach after having reviewed some intrinsic limitations of the proposed approach. [SOURCE: Page_27]

II.1 Health risks in reuse chain Irrigation with treated wastewater may have benefits in terms of providing nutrients to crops, mainly with three types of organic components that the wastewater contains: car- bon, nitrogen and phosphorus. But it can also have disadvantages, because it also contains various contaminants: as present at trace state, called ‘micropollutants’. This term refers [SOURCE: Page_27]

11 [SOURCE: Page_28]

Figure 2: The current situation, vision of the future and its ideal implementation for maxi- mizing the recovery of resources in WRRF [SOURCE: Page_28]

12 [SOURCE: Page_29]

to a group of mineral or organic substances which can be toxic, even at very low con- centrations of the order of micro or nano-grams per liter [Shakir et al. 2017]. They are used during industrial processes or are part of the composition of many agricultural or domestic products. These molecules can be grouped into several categories: pesticides, pharmaceutical or veterinary residues, organic compounds derived from plastics, etc. The reused wastewater also contains some pathogens (virus, fungi, bacteria etc) and emerging contaminants as antibiotic resistant genes which can render bacteria resistant. Therefore, the transfer of infections by these categories of contaminants is one of the main issues in the reuse of treated wastewater. In the following Section , we present some important limitations related to the health risks and environmental impacts. [SOURCE: Page_29]

II.2 Reuse limitations The challenge of the reuse of treated wastewater in agriculture is double: on the one hand the preservation of water resources, and on the other hand the recovery of nutrients (in particular nitrogen and phosphorus) for crops or industrial usages. However, the reuse of treated wastewater for agriculture has many limitations that place it under a certain restriction such as : [SOURCE: Page_29]

• The presence of dissolved salts in water can have several agronomic consequences. As the salinity increases, the osmotic gradient between soil water and plant root cells decreases. The plant must therefore expend more energy to recover water, which affects its growth [Tchobanoglous et al. 2007]. [SOURCE: Page_29]

• The irrigation system used may have difficulties in dealing with loaded wastewa- ters. The fact of using such quality-tailored waters and thus the characteristics of treated wastewater can promote fouling through biofilm formation and/or chemical precipitation, notably when drip irrigation is used, [Lequette et al. 2019]. [SOURCE: Page_29]

• The Nutrient contained in treated wastewater provide fertilizer value for crop pro- duction, but in some cases their form is proper to readily infiltrated into the soil, which can affect the quality of groundwater. [Candela et al. 2007]. [SOURCE: Page_29]

II.3 Limitations of the proposed approach The innovative approach we propose presents a number of limitations that will deserve future work. Among these limitations, it should be underlined that if there are many models of plants describing the needs for water, there are very few models involving their needs in terms of nitrogen compounds and even less with respect to phosphorus or other compounds of interest. In addition, it is particularly difficult to propose optimization criterion that could be considered universal: with respect to which objective the innovative approach we propose should be optimal to? In the absence of obvious indicators and criteria to evaluate the performances, how to evaluate the reuse of treated water in terms of cost, performance and environmental impacts? Finally, what exactly flexibility stands for within the framework of this study? [SOURCE: Page_29]

13 [SOURCE: Page_30]

III. Aims and Objectives [SOURCE: Page_30]

All around the world, treated or untreated wastewater is reused for irrigation of plants and crops [Jiménez & Asano 2008]. Treated wastewaters are widely reused for irrigation in semi-arid countries by necessity. The concept of reclaiming wastewater for reuse in agri- culture promotes a shift from traditional irrigation to fertigation [Chojnacka et al. 2020], where both irrigation and fertilization must satisfy the requirements of the plants/crops. Several approaches are developed for resources recovery and reuse for agriculture pur- poses, but the concept of adapting wastewater treatment to the plants/crops requirements for reuse is new and thus rarely studied. An analysis of the current situation of water management and a review of advanced irrigation and wastewater treatment practices highlight the need for flexible efficient treat- ment wastewater facilities that considers wastewater as a water resource that can be reused in adapting its quality to the requirements of plants/crops. The objective of this work is to develop and study such strategies for the operation of a wastewater treatment system to produce water of adaptable quality that can be reused in agriculture. We propose an innovative approach that involves the use of a new concept of flexible wastewater treatment system, from which the system is dynamically controlled to operate according to demand. Therefore, the approach schematically represented in 3 is to use a first model that allows to identify (or to estimate using an observer) the water and/or nitrogen needs of the crop/plant, which is then used as setpoints within a flexible WRRF. In a closed loop, the flexible system will be dynamically controlled to meet the needs required by the plant/crop model. The optimal nitrogen requirement for crop is used to adapt the treatment in the flexible system and provide the necessary quality for optimal growth of the crops. Additionally, this flexible system must be capable of providing several qualities of treated wastewater to meet irrigation needs during periods of water stress ( nutrients recov- ery in the treated water for reuse), but also to comply with discharging constraints outside of irrigation periods. In addition, it must be controlled in such a way the transitions periods between these two operating modes remain as small as possible. In this concept the transi- tion dynamics between these two modes of operation are studied in order to determine the best combinations of the control degrees to minimize the response time of a transition (the time needed to switch from one mode to the other). Furthermore, to adapt the output of the WRRF to plant needs, it is necessary to close the loop: since it is difficult to online estimate the water needs for the plant, we developed a method, based on the use of unknown input observers, to estimate plant needs in terms of water (more precisely we estimate the evapotranspiration of the plants) using measurements of soil water content. Finally, to optimize the management of MBR and improve performances of such sys- tem, we developed optimal control algorithms to maximize the water produced over a given period of time while minimizing the required energy. [SOURCE: Page_30]

14 [SOURCE: Page_31]

Figure 3: Our strategy for close the loop between the WRRF model and the Corps/plants model [SOURCE: Page_31]

The PhD thesis is organized as follows. The first chapter is a literature review that provides a general introduction of biological pathways to treat carbon and nitrogen present in wastewater, beginning with a brief introduction to aerobic treatment systems and the main processes used in conventional treatment with a description of their concept. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Chapter II describes the development of a simulation flexible platform able to be controlled in such a way to adapt the treatment according to requirements of plants/crops. This platform is analyzed numerically with respect to its possible functioning configura- tions, each of them representing an operating mode. Depending on the configurations of its actuators, the steady states are characterized. Additionally, an advanced control system is proposed based on the implementation of simple robust industrial controllers in cascade (PI). In terms of control design, several objectives are then pursued: by manipulating the continuous available actuators, we first show how controlling the output nitrate concentration SN O$_3$ and then the ratio SN O$_3$ /SN H in effluent. The requirements of crops used (the setpoints) is the optimal solution for the problem of maximizing crop biomass at the end of a growing season. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Chapter III presents the study of the time needed to switch from one functioning mode to another. The objective of this part is to analyze the best combination of control degrees for each operation mode, and to use these different degrees of freedom to minimize the time response to go from one mode to another. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Chapter IV is a preliminary study for estimating the water needed by a culture, un- der weather disturbances, from the measurement of the soil water content (SW C). This work is intended to optimize irrigation supply by providing guidelines to growers and farmers on sustainable water management. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Finally, chapter V presents a methodology to synthesize an optimal control feed- back for membrane filtration system in order to improve the performances of filtration in wastewater system. Given the high price of the water produced for REUSE, the pursued [SOURCE: Page_31]

15 [SOURCE: Page_32]

objective is to optimize the whole process in minimizing the energy consumption under performance constraints. [SOURCE: Page_32]

The reader being familiar with the modeling of bioprocesses for environmental pro- cesses and the basis of control systems may directly go to chapter 2. [SOURCE: Page_32]

18                       Chapter 1. Modeling and control of a WRRF for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_35]

1.1       Aerobic treatment systems 1.1.1     Biological wastewater treatment process A wastewater treatment plant, named WWTP, typically uses a combination of mechanical, physical, chemical and biological processes to remove pollutants from the influent wastew- ater. The activated sludge process is the most commonly used water treatment process. The biological treatment process removes major organic matter - carbon and/or nitrogen and phosphorus - from wastewater by means of microorganisms. In what follows we restrict attention to carbon and nitrogen removal. The biodegradable organic matter is oxidized by the micro-organisms and carbon dioxide is produced. Biological nitrogen removal is a two-step process that involves nitrification and denitrification. The activated sludge (floc- culated biomass) is gravitationally separated from the treated wastewater in the secondary settler, and most of it is returned to the biological process through a recirculation circuit. Several process configurations exist. [SOURCE: Page_35]

1.1.2     Conventional activated sludge processes The characteristics of an activated sludge process depend on the flow regime in the reactor, the size, the number and the configuration of the reactors used together with the recycle flows and the influent parameters, i.e. the environmental conditions or system constraints under which biological processes are constrained to operate. From the large variety of nitrification/denitrification activated sludge processes, three basic configurations are most frequently used in practice. [SOURCE: Page_35]

1.1.2.1    Pre-denitrification process [SOURCE: Page_35]

[Ludzack & Ettinger 1962] has proposed a nitrification-denitrification process taking place simultaneously in a single tank. [Barnard 1973] proposed a modification of the Ludzack- Ettinger system by completely separating the anoxic and aerobic reactors to allow a pre- denitrification process Fig.1.1 to take place. In this process, the denitrification step, which takes place in the anoxic reactor is placed upstream of the aerobic reactor of nitrification. Wastewater coming into the process firstly passes through the denitrification step such as the organic matter contained in the wastewater is used as a source of carbon for denitrifica- tion. The ammonia is oxidized into nitrate in the final aerobic stage, and the nitrified liquor is recycled to the denitrification step by an internal recirculation. [SOURCE: Page_35]

1.1.2.2    Post-denitrification process [SOURCE: Page_35]

In a post-denitrification process (cf. Fig.1.2), the nitrification step of the aerobic reactor is placed before denitrification process. The influent is discharged to the aerobic reactor where aerobic growth of both the heterotrophic and nitrifying organisms take place while nitrification is completed in the first reactor. Therefore, sufficient sludge age is allowed. The mixed liquor from the aerobic reactor passes through the anoxic reactor. Most of the [SOURCE: Page_35]

### 1.1. Aerobic treatment systems                                                              19

Figure 1.1: Pre-denitrification process [SOURCE: Page_36]

carbon source is consumed during nitrification. Consequently, an external source of carbon is required in the denitrification process. [SOURCE: Page_36]

Figure 1.2: Post-denitrification process [SOURCE: Page_36]

1.1.2.3   Step-feed process [SOURCE: Page_36]

This process was developed to improve the performance of pre-denitrification pro- cesses. The cost of pumping power for recycling the mixed liquor is very high for pre- denitrification process [Miyaji et al. 1981], in particular for a large scale wastewater treat- ment plant. The modification consists in creating a multi-stage process in which the denitri- fication and the nitrification compartments are arranged alternatively in series. Wastewater is equally divided into each of the denitrification compartments (cf. Fig1.3). Wastewater flow through the nitrification and denitrification compartments, successively while a lim- ited part of the mixed liquor from the final nitrification compartment is recycled to the first denitrification compartment. [SOURCE: Page_36]

20                      Chapter 1. Modeling and control of a WRRF for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_37]

Figure 1.3: Step-feed process [SOURCE: Page_37]

1.1.3   Innovative activated sludge processes Recent ‘innovative activated sludge processes’ have been proposed. They are advanced versions of the activated sludge process, aiming at improving performance relatively to a conventional process in terms of effluent quality while reducing operating costs. Several upgrading process designs for wastewater treatment plants have been developed (for in- stance Table.1.1), in response to changes in treatment needs and advances in understanding of process. These configuration processes have been used basically to maintain pollutant discharges within the restrictions that protect water resources. [SOURCE: Page_37]

1.1.4   Control of wastewater treatment plants Automatic control is used in various industrial processes, including wastewater treatment. To control a process, we must be able to ‘act on it’ with a control signal. Generally, the aim of control is to maintain one or several outputs of the process at desired value(s), despite disturbances. To do so, a fundamental concept is to use feedback using the available knowledge (measurements) or estimates of these outputs to compute the input signal - the control. There are several general incentives to use control strategies in wastewater treatment plants: [SOURCE: Page_37]

• Meeting a strict effluent demand. [SOURCE: Page_37]

• Dampening the impact of process disturbances, as the load to a wastewater treatment plant usually shows large variations. [SOURCE: Page_37]

• Achieving a cost-effective solution, i.e. optimizing the operation in order to mini- mize operating costs. [SOURCE: Page_37]

The first step in the design of an automatic control system is to determine which are the available input and output signals. In a WWTP, many variables can be used to control nitrogen removal: Control of hydraulic variables : [SOURCE: Page_37]

22                       Chapter 1. Modeling and control of a WRRF for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_39]

• Control of internal recirculation rate : it allows to recirculate nitrite/nitrate from the aerated reactor to the anoxic one to denitrify. As a consequence, this affects both the denitrification and nitrification processes, or more precisely the nitrate and ammonium nitrogen concentrations in the effluent. [SOURCE: Page_39]

• The sludge recycling flow rate: the control of the return sludge flow rate affects the concentration of return sludge and mixed liquor in the tanks. Practically, this con- trol is designed to maintain a constant biomass concentration in the tanks. Incorrect flow control will either - to clogging the filtration system (secondary clarify or mem- brane) by solids and bacteria when the sludge return flow is minimal - or will result in reduced solids deposited in the filtration system and the return of sludge, which contains more water [SOURCE: Page_39]

• Excess sludge flow rate: It is an important control in the sludge activated process because the wasting rate determines the solid retention time (SRT) Which, in return, affects the bacteria growth rate, oxygen consumption, nitrification activity and final effluent quality. [SOURCE: Page_39]

Control of physical variables: [SOURCE: Page_39]

• Dissolved Oxygen : oxygen transfer coefficient is manipulated for controlling the concentration of dissolved oxygen in order to control the ammonium concentration resulting from nitrification. [SOURCE: Page_39]

• The external carbon source : when there is a lack of readily biodegradable substrate in influent, or when a post denitrifying configuration is used, a control of the external carbon source is used to control the denitrification. [SOURCE: Page_39]

1.1.5    A new outlook for wastewaters Within the framework of circular economy, wastewater is regarded as a source of energy, nutrients and water and not only as a source of pollution. The paradigm shift from wastew- ater treatment plants towards water resource recovery facilities (WRRFs) - that we already mentioned in the introduction - creates a challenge to design, modeling and control new plants, by changing treatment needs and objectives, starting from nutrients recovery and water reuse to energy recovery and sustainability. The selection of the system design to be used, as well as its modes of operation, will depend on some key factors: [SOURCE: Page_39]

• WRRF effluent requirements have a direct effect on the selection of the process to achieve specific treated water quality, energy and sustainability goals and objec- tives. Conventional technologies generally tend to increase treatment efficiency, by improving the performance of nitrogen and carbon removal process to best achieve discharge standards. [SOURCE: Page_39]

### 1.1. Aerobic treatment systems                                                            23

1.1.6   Modeling concept for activated sludge systems [SOURCE: Page_40]

Activated sludge bioreactors are very complex systems regarding hydraulics, biochemical reactions and because they are subject to input disturbance, that is the variability in the composition of influent wastewater. Activated sludge bioreactors are therefore very diffi- cult to model and a significant number of simplifications need to be made before construct- ing a feasible activated sludge bioreactor model that can be simulated. Activated sludge bioreactors come in different forms, sizes and configurations. In this thesis, only a small part of them is considered, i.e. the activated sludge reactors and the immersed membrane reactor (without taking into account the fouling in the platform proposed) both assumed to be continuously fed. For general guidance, the reader can refer to [Eddy et al. 2003]. [SOURCE: Page_40]

1.1.7   Bioreactor hydraulics [SOURCE: Page_40]

Flow patterns by means of continuous flow reactors are very complex resulting from complicated tanks geometry and the location and design of the inlets, outlets, mixers and aeration devices. According to these characteristics as well as different operating conditions, such as liquid and air flow rates and mixing intensities, reactors can present a certain range of undesirable hydraulic conditions [Kjellstrand 2006]. Hydraulic conditions affect the residence time distributions of the liquid and solid phases in the mixed liquor. [SOURCE: Page_40]

Large-scale bioreactors rarely exhibit a perfectly mixed nor ideal plug flow behav- iors, the internal flow pattern generally falling ‘somewhere in between’. These conditions, nor completely mixed nor plugged flow, are generally modeled with a cascade of reactors as represented in Fig.1.4. When increasing the number of reactors in the cascade, the distribution of residence times in the cascade tends to appear as a plug flow and eventually reaches an ideal plug flow when N → ∞. The cascade bioreactor structure can be adjusted by varying the number, the volumes and the sequence of completely stirred tank reactors (CSTR), recycling rate, addition of sidestream tanks to represent dead zones, introducing by-passes to represent short circuits, etc. [SOURCE: Page_40]

The topology identification of the ‘series tank’ model is typically performed ini- tially by identifying recirculation zones, dead zones and short circuits in the physical system via tracer tests, i.e. the temporal response to the pulse or step change in the concentration of an introduced substance in the influent, and then fitting the response curve of the mathematical ‘series tank’ model to the measurements[Olsson & Newell 1999]. Experimental studies in the identification of structures of wastewater treatment plant models using the ‘series tank’ approach show that the structure of connections between reactors is often subject to change with operating conditions (influent flow rate, aeration rate, mixing, etc.) [Alex et al. 2002]. [SOURCE: Page_40]

Depending on the objectives to be achieved and the characteristics of the influent to be treated, very different situations may be encountered and the literature shows that in general, under WWTP operating conditions, plug flow reactors are the most efficient [SOURCE: Page_40]

24                       Chapter 1. Modeling and control of a WRRF for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_41]

[de Gooijer et al. 1996] [Scuras et al. 2001]. Moreover, reactors perfectly mixed in series of volumes sized according to the influent with a hydraulic retention time (HRT) capable of ensuring a desired treatment performance are generally sufficient to correctly approximate the input-output behaviour of a plug-flow reactor, hence the configurations encountered in real systems and in associated benchmarks, such as the BSM$_1$. [SOURCE: Page_41]

Figure 1.4: Graphical representation of a cascade of N CSTR, the concentration variations over time in response to a pulse are shown in the top [SOURCE: Page_41]

Each bioreactor in Fig.1.4 is described by the general mass balance equation : [SOURCE: Page_41]

d          X           X (CV ) =   Sources −   Sinks                               (1.1) dt where C is the vector of the concentrations of various components in the bioreactor, and V is the liquid phase volume, which is constant in a CSTR. The concentration C of each component inside the bioreactor is decreased by sinks and increased by sources. In the bioreactors described in this thesis, these sinks and sources are attributed to the mass flow of liquid with main outflow and inflow, mass flow of air (e.g. aeration) and a secondary outflow (e.g. waste activated sludge (WAS)). The secondary inflows (e.g. as for instance chemical dosing of external carbon for denitrification) are not considered. If aeration and chemical dosing are not taken into account, the mass balance equation for any CSTR can be expressed as follows: [SOURCE: Page_41]

d (CV ) = Qin Cin − Qout Cout ± rV                          (1.2) dt C, Cin and Cout being the vectors of concentrations of all considered wastewater con- stituents in the influent, in the reactor and in the effluent, respectively; r is the vector of reaction rates and dim(r) = dim(C); V is the active volume of the reactor; Qin and Qout are the influent and effluent flow rates. Keeping in mind that in a completely stirred tank reactor (CSTR) Cout = C and Qin = Qout , the mass balance of a CSTR can be expressed with the following ordinary differen- tial equations (ODE). dC     Qin =      (Cin − C) ± r                               (1.3) dt     V [SOURCE: Page_41]

### 1.2. Activated Sludge Models (ASMs)                                                        25

1.1.8   Modeling biochemical reactions The kinetics of biochemical processes describe the rate at which the biomass grows - as a function of the state variables and in particular as a function of substrates concentrations. Substrates are supposed to be transformed into products and biomass in a single step with- out any consideration of the complex reactions that occur at a single cellular level. The macroscopic models do not take into neither account any variation in the composition and activity of individual cells (and thus often group different bacterial species into a single type of biomass). It is characterized by its concentration X(gm−3 ), its maximum growth rate µ̂(d−1 ), its decay rate kD (d−1 ), a yield coefficient Y (−). depending on the function used to model its growth, it includes other kinetics and stoichiometric parameters such as, e.g. Monod constants K for different substrates. [SOURCE: Page_42]

1.2     Activated Sludge Models (ASMs) The first period of activated sludge models development was from the 1950 to 1960. [Eckenfelder Jr & Porges 1957] proposed simple, empirical equations to estimate the amount of excess sludge produced and oxygen demand in the tank. The second phase of activated sludge models development can be characterized as the formal application of chemical reaction-type kinetics to link (at steady state) microbial growth and the use of the organic substrate under aerobic conditions [Wanner & Jobbágy 2014]. In 1982 the In- ternational Water Quality Association (IWAQ) promoted the formation of an international working group in charge of accelerating the development of dynamic modeling of wastew- ater treatment plants, to create a common platform of agreement and to propose a simple starting model. The group focused its first efforts on inventorying existing models and the constraints to their development. It was concluded that there were a large number of dynamic models, the diffusion of which was hampered by their complexity, lack of or- der in their presentation and the limits of computer power. The results of the group were published in 1987 in a report that presented the model known today as the ASM1 model [Henze et al. 1987], a model designed to describe degradation of organic matter, nitrifica- tion and denitrification in an activated sludge process. The most important achievements of the group were a consensus on the biological processes that make up the model, the nor- malization of symbols, the presentation of the model using matrix notation, the proposal of ‘default’ values for the model parameters and the adoption of COD and its fraction to characterize wastewater. In 1995, the work of the study group was extended to publish the ASM2 version, which is aimed at predicting the behavior of phosphorus. In 1999 there were two additional modifications with the appearance of the ASM2d version (modified [SOURCE: Page_42]

26                       Chapter 1. Modeling and control of a WRRF for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_43]

version of ASM$_2$) [Henze et al. 1999], and the ASM$_3$ version which was designed to pro- vide a response to phenomena that ASM$_1$ was unable to simulate, such as reserve substrate storage, for periods of absence of substrate. Such models are intended to be capable of pre- dicting the performance of single-reactor of activated sludge systems and to be used as a tool by engineers for process design and optimization of activated sludge wastewater treat- ment plants (WWTPs). Even though models mentioned above are the most widely known ASMs to the technical and scientific communities, a number of other activated sludge mod- els have been published and successfully tested in a number of studies. [SOURCE: Page_43]

1.2.1   Structure Activated Sludge Models (ASMs) The activated sludge models represent a matrix structure that will be presented here with more details. The vector of reaction rates, r ∈ Rn in Equation 1.2 and Equation 1.3 takes the form:                      r1 (Ĉ1 ⊆ C, u)  r=         ..       (1.4)           .        rn (Ĉn ⊆ C, u) rj where j = 1 : n represents the rate of change of the concentration of the j-th component Cj as a result of biological and chemical reactions that occur inside the reactor. C is the vector of concentrations of soluble and particulate components, including bacterial biomass. Ĉj is the vector of concentrations which are used as arguments in the rate equation rj . u is the vector of external inputs. The number of reaction rates n is equal to the number of unknown concentrations in order to form a closed system of equation required to verify the existence of a single solution to a system of algebraic equations. Each component Cj is a substrate or a product for one or more biological or chemical processes, such as denitrification, nitrification, hydrolysis, etc. These processes p ∈ Rm can be represented as :                                                           p1 (C̆1 ⊆ C, u) p=           ..       (1.5)            .        pm (C̆m ⊆ C, u) with m is the number of processes. Each reaction rate rj for component j can be expressed as a linear combination of several process rate pi m X ∀j ∈< 1, n >: rj =          ai,j pi                      (1.6) i=1 Equation 1.6 can also be written in the matrix form as : r = AT p                                      (1.7) where A ∈ Rm×n is the matrix of stoichiometric parameters, also known as the ‘Petersen’ or ‘Gujer’ matrix                                     a1,1 · · · a1,n  .     ..      ..  A=    ..       .     .                             (1.8) am,1 · · · am,n [SOURCE: Page_43]

### 1.2. Activated Sludge Models (ASMs)                                                         27

Each stoichiometric parameter ai,j in matrix A is the link between the rate of the i-th process pi and the rate of change of the concentration of the j-th component Cj due to that process. If ai,j > 0 then the component Cj is a product, if ai,j < 0 then the component Cj is a substrate, if ai,j = 0 the component Cj does not take part in that process. In simulations of a CSTR, the vector of state variables x ∈ Rn is equal to the vector of concentrations in the bulk liquid, x = C. Thus, if we replace C with x, then ẋ = r and Equation 1.7 can be expressed as : ẋ = AT p                                      (1.9) Each process p ∈ p must satisfy two principal laws of chemistry : the law of conservation of mass (matter) and the law of conservation of energy. The law of conservation of matter stipulates that in an ordinary chemical reaction, the mass of the products is equal to the mass of the reactants. Matter cannot be created or destroyed, although it can be reorganized. The law of conservation of energy stipulates that energy cannot be created or destroyed, but can change its form. In the context of activated sludge models (ASMs), the conservation of these two fundamental laws necessitates that, in the most general form, each process p conserves the mass of carbon (C), nitrogen (N) and phosphorus (P) and net electrical charge. The amounts of C, N, P and electrical charge in all components are written in a mass and charge conservation matrix. The quantities of C, N, P and electrical charge in all components are written in a mass and charge conservation matrix :                   I$_1$,1 · · · I$_1$,n  .    ..      ..  I$_4$×n =  ..           .    .                               (1.10) I$_4$,1 · · · I$_4$,n where Wi Ii,j is the quantities of C, N, P and electrical charge for i= 1, 2, 3 and 4 respec- tively. for the j-th component. Each reaction r ∈ r must satisfy all four balance equations, which imposes restrictions on the choice of stoichiometric parameters ai,j ∈ Am×n . In order to satisfy all balance equations, the following matrix equation must be satisfied. [SOURCE: Page_44]

Am×n (I$_4$×n )T ≈ 0m×4                                 (1.11) [SOURCE: Page_44]

For the purpose to satisfy the equation 1.11, on k non-zero stoichiometric parameters in the matrix A, k − 4m parameters are selected manually and the others, i.e. 4m parameters must be calculated by solving the equation 1.11 to ensure the conservation and continuity of mass and charge. [SOURCE: Page_44]

1.2.2   Reaction kinetics The processes p ∈ p in Equation 1.4 represent different biochemical enzymatic reactions carried out by different types of bacteria in the activated sludge process. These reactions are linked to the consumption of substrates, the production of products, the consumption or release of energy and the growth or degradation of biomass. In a general form, the rate of a process with a single substrate and a single bacterial biomass without consideration of temperature variation is expressed as : [SOURCE: Page_44]

p(X, S) = mµ(X, S)                                  (1.12) [SOURCE: Page_44]

28                        Chapter 1. Modeling and control of a WRRF for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_45]

where p(X, S)(gm−3 d−1 ) is the process rate, m(d−1 ) is the maximum growth rate; µ(X, S) is the process rate dependency function on substrate (S) and biomass (X) con- centrations; In biotechnology and in the ASM models, the process rates p(X, S) are proportional to the biomass concentration X(gm−3 ) and thus µ(X, S) = µ(S)X. Equation 1.12 then takes the form as : k(X, S) = mµ(S)X                               (1.13) The value of p(X, S) is lower than the maximum process rate m because of the limitation substrate effects, diffusion effects, inhibition, competition for different types of bacteria to same substrate, etc. These effects are accounted for in a non-dimensional function µ(S) < 1 , which may take one of the following forms as shown in Table. 1.2 [SOURCE: Page_45]

Model                Kinetics expression µ(S) 1   1st order kinetics   S S 2   Monod                KS +S SR 3   Moser                KS0R +S R S 4   Haldane              S$_2$ Ki +S+K   H [SOURCE: Page_45]

Table 1.2: Reaction kinetics dependent on single substrate concentration [SOURCE: Page_45]

With KS , KS$_0$ and KH denote the half-saturation constant respectively for Monod, Moser and Haldane equation and Ki is the inhibition constant. Monod kinetics is a specific case of Moser kinetics where R = 1. The population dynamics of bacterial species in ASM models consider two opposite mechanisms: growth and decay. The net growth of a bacterial species which considers biomass growth, maintenance, decay, and lysis is calculated as superposition of two oppos- ing mechanisms as shown in Equation 1.14: [SOURCE: Page_45]

p(X, S) = mµ(S)X − kD X                                 (1.14) [SOURCE: Page_45]

With kD (d−1 ) is the bacterial decay coefficient. [SOURCE: Page_45]

1.2.3    Activated Sludge Model No. 1 The Activated Sludge Model No. 1 (ASM1) is the most widely accepted biological wastew- ater treatment model. It is widely used to model aerobic WWTP that perform organic car- bon oxidation, nitrification and denitrification. The model does not describe phosphorus removal. The ASM1 considers two types of bacteria (heterotrophs and autotrophs) and is based on eight fundamental biological processes : Anoxic growth of heterotrophs, Aerobic growth of heterotrophs, Aerobic growth of autotrophs, Decay of heterotrophs, Decay of autotrophs, Ammonification of soluble organic nitrogen, Hydrolysis of entrapped organics and Hydrolysis of entrapped organic nitrogen. In this model, the chemical oxygen demand and total nitrogen (COD and TN, respectively) are decomposed into several state variables, ‘according to physico-chemical criteria (particulate, soluble) and biodegradability (inert, [SOURCE: Page_45]

### 1.2. Activated Sludge Models (ASMs)                                                         29

rapidly or slowly biodegradable)’. For the COD fractions, we consider the soluble readily biodegradable matter SS , the soluble inert SI , the particulate slowly biodegradable matter XS , the particulate inert XI , the particulate inert from biomass decay XP , the heterotrophic biomass XBH and the autotrophic biomass XBA . For the fractions of TN, we consider the soluble organic SN D , the ammoniacal nitrogen SN H . The particulate organics are XN D , nitrates and nitrites SN O ; and two other state variables, the oxygen SO and the alkalin- ity SALK . The model includes 19 stoichiometric and kinetic constants which are used as parameters for the process rate equations. Figure 1.5 illustrates the biological processes used in ASM$_1$. Therefore, in aerobic conditions, heterotrophs (XBH ) oxidize the readily biodegradable organic substrate (SS ) to carbon dioxide, while autotrophs allow the oxidation of ammoniacal nitrogen (SN H ) into nitrite and nitrate (SN O ). Subsequently, part of the active biomass (XBH and XBA ) is subjected to cell lysis generating biodegradable organic (XS ) and nitrogen (XN D ) frac- tions, hence the concept of dead-regeneration, and an inert fraction (XP ). The particulate fractions (XS and XN D ) are then hydrolyzed to produce soluble organic SS and soluble organic nitrogen (SN D ). Although ASM$_1$ has proved to work well in a wide range of ap- [SOURCE: Page_46]

Figure 1.5: Overview of ASM$_1$ processes [SOURCE: Page_46]

plications for the original purposes for which it was designed, such as predicting sludge production, oxygen demand and effluent quality, the model has certain limitations: [SOURCE: Page_46]

30                       Chapter 1. Modeling and control of a WRRF for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_47]

• It does not describe the restrictive effects of alkalinity as well as N, P and other inorganic nutrients on the growth of biomass; [SOURCE: Page_47]

• It divides particulate organic matter into two separate compartments : from the in- fluent (XI ) and biomass decomposition (XP ). In reality, these two COD fractions cannot be separated one from each other; [SOURCE: Page_47]

• The combined effects of death, predation, biomass lysis, and endogenous respira- tion of storage products are described as a combined effect of lysis, hydrolysis, and growth mechanisms, but not modeled individually. [SOURCE: Page_47]

1.2.4    A modified Activated Sludge Model ASM$_1$-2ND Within the framework of water reuse, it may be important to consider the different forms of nitrogen. Since nitrite is an intermediate product, which can in principle be easily oxidized into nitrate, most activated sludge models consider nitrification as a single step process such as ASM$_1$ model 1.7. The assumption of one-step nitrification is generally accept- able when modeling classical treatment processes. However, the treatment plant operation may reveal nitrite accumulation due to incorrect design or ineffective operation. Therefore, it is interesting to examine both nitrification and denitrification as a two-step processes. The ASM$_1$-2ND model [Ostace et al. 2011] is developed as an enhanced ASM$_1$ with two step nitrification/denitrification processes^1.6. The principal components of the modified model (ASM$_1$-2ND) remain the same as the components of the ASM$_1$ model. The total chemical oxygen demand (COD) is divided into three categories: Biodegradable COD, non-biodegradable COD and active biomass. But the nitrogen components are modified, since nitrification is modeled as a two-step process: the nitrate and nitrite concentrations, represented in a single variable (SN O ) in the ASM$_1$ are represented in the ASM$_1$-2ND by two different state variables (SN O$_2$ and SN O$_3$ ), where the SN O$_2$ component is the interme- diate for the nitrification and denitrification processes. The autotrophic bacteria (XBA ) of ASM$_1$ is also divided in two biomass types: a group oxidizes ammonium to nitrite: these are the Nitrosomonas bacteria (XAOB ), and a group oxidizes nitrite to nitrate: these are the Nitrobacter bacteria (XN OB ). [SOURCE: Page_47]

1.3      Towards a flexible water resource recovery facility (WRRF) As the industry moves towards resource recovery, including water, energy, and nutrients, several initiatives are promoting this new name (Water Resource Recovery Facilities - WRRF) for wastewater treatment plants (WWTPs). The wastewater treatment technol- ogy progress to provide more advanced treatment processes, and more technical advances is included to conventional WWTP as: [SOURCE: Page_47]

• Advances in Membrane Bioreactor Applications [SOURCE: Page_47]

• Use and application of wastewater treatment process modeling for the development of new designs and evaluation of alternatives [SOURCE: Page_47]

### 1.3. Towards a flexible water resource recovery facility (WRRF)                         31

32                       Chapter 1. Modeling and control of a WRRF for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_49]

• Increasing goals toward energy recovery [SOURCE: Page_49]

• Trend toward resource recovery [SOURCE: Page_49]

The objective of flexibility, in terms of water resource recovery facilities, is to adapt their functioning to short-term variations in requirements. The sources or levers of flexibil- ity can be : [SOURCE: Page_49]

• The configuration changes, by means of switches (as valves), the flexible configu- ration can result in several sub-configurations, of which each sub-configuration is useful for one operating mode. [SOURCE: Page_49]

• Through the introduction of additional control levers, the system can achieve perfor- mance levels in terms of quality not possible with the classic configuration, which contains fewer degrees of control, and it allows to optimize the operation and giving more flexibility. [SOURCE: Page_49]

The proposed flexible platform is an upgraded design of the conventional wastewater treatment plants (WWTPs). The idea of the design is to build a configuration that contains several control degrees in order to be able to give the plant more flexibility and overcome some limits that the conventional WWTPs. The platform is designed to provide efficient control of quality and quantity of the nitrogen forms at its output, to allow an optimal reuse of treated water in agriculture. The particularity of this platform lies in the fact that it is able to operate in different configurations, thanks to the available valves (considered to have only two positions 0 or 1). They allow the platform to be operated in several given configurations. Since the effluent is intended for reuse, a high quality filtration is necessary: the platform includes a membrane which is supposed to be ideal to ensure perfect sludge separation (fouling phenomena were not taken into account). For a more detailed presentation, the reader will refer to the next chapter. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Remark 1.3.1. When working on control of complex systems, two cases arise: either the complex system is a real system either it is a virtual process. Within the framework of this manuscript, the flexible platform is not a model for a real process but considered as a ‘toy WWRF’ (a virtual process for us) to be controlled. As such, we will consider its model as unknown: it is why it will not be considered for control synthesis. In addition, to facilitate the implementation of the proposed control strategies on a pilot/real system, we will concentrate on the design of simple industrial control algorithm instead of on the synthesis of ‘advanced control systems’ that would require complex models which the development may be a long and tedious task. [SOURCE: Page_49]

1.4      Modeling of plants/crops needs An important benefit of reuse is fertilization through irrigation (fertigation), as wastewater contains nitrogen compounds, which are essential plant nutrients. Therefore, special attention is given to how nitrogen dynamics are taken into account in crop models. [SOURCE: Page_49]

### 1.4. Modeling of plants/crops needs                                                          33

Crop models are tools developed to estimate crop yields as a function of plant char- acteristics, weather, soil conditions and applied management practices. These models represent farming systems at a large scale (at least a field) and simulate growth and development of a crop growing on a uniform area of land. [SOURCE: Page_50]

A wide range of models have been developed over the years, from empirical to mechanistic and for various different purposes (educational, scientific, commercial) [Jones et al. 2017]. There are few models that have been developed to be parsimonious and that are also formulated clearly as dynamical systems. Generally, they have been established specifically for control or optimization purposes, as simplifications of more complex models. Only one of these models includes nitrogen dynamics. This model, called the crop-soil model [Pelak et al. 2017], has been developed as a simplified version of AquaCrop, a standard simulation model of the FAO (Food and Agriculture Organization of the UN), The crop-soil model [Pelak et al. 2017] is proposed following the ideas of AquaCrop, where soil moisture and nitrogen balance are considered. Crops are mainly represented by the canopy cover (the fraction of ground covered by the crop), the other variables being the vertically averaged relative soil moisture and nitrogen content per unit area of soil, for which balance equations are established. Accumulated biomass can then be computed as a function of these variables. This model [Pelak et al. 2017] has been used to study an optimal irrigation problem, that is the maximization of biomass production at harvest, without any constraint on the total amount of water. The study was realized as part of Control^4Reuse project (cf. control^4reuse.net). The only adaptation for the considered problem is to take the fertilization term as function of the product of the irrigation flow-rate per unit area and the nitrogen concentration of the irrigation water. In this context, to determine the irrigation and fertilization requirements for optimal crop growth, a problem of maximizing crop biomass at the end of the growing season is considered. This as an optimal control problem, over a fixed time horizon that represents a growing season. The solutions of this optimal problem is used as the optimal nitrogen requirements for crops irrigation, that is as a setpoint for the simulation platform. [SOURCE: Page_50]

In this thesis, we will not discuss the modeling of plants/crops, but we will take the solutions found from the problem of optimal irrigation for nitrogen needs (solution of the problem of maximizing biomass production) solved in another research context and use it as references in our treatment system in order to control it such as it is able to provide water of appropriate quality to the plants/crops and ensure optimal production (for more details visit the appendix III). [SOURCE: Page_50]

The Water balance is the application of the principle of conservation of water con- tained in a given volume of soil. The water content of the soil during a growing season changes according to the crop, which can draw a certain amount of water from a reservoir whose water content varies over time, mainly depending on rainfall and actual evapotran- spiration. [SOURCE: Page_50]

34                      Chapter 1. Modeling and control of a WRRF for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_51]

The water balance Fig.1.8 equation is based on the continuity equation and can be expressed as follows, for a given period and a given basin: [SOURCE: Page_51]

P + I − ET − Dr − Ro ± ∆Q = 0                             (1.15) where P is precipitation, I irrigation, ET evapotranspiration, Dr drainage, Ro runoff, and ∆Q the variation of soil water content during a given irrigation interval. [SOURCE: Page_51]

We are interested in this part of thesis only by the management of water resources for irrigation. The modeling consists in writing a dynamic system which allows to identify water resources in the soil: a soil model that provides information on the water state on the system from which the plant takes the necessary water resource for its growth. [SOURCE: Page_51]

1.5    Modeling and control of fouling in membrane filtration sys- tems Operating a WRRF for Water reuse including a membrane reactor has an important draw- back: the energy needed for operating the system will have as a consequence to increase the price of the treated water. To render reuse economically viable, it is thus particularly important to optimize the whole process and, in particular, the way the membrane system is operated in order to minimize fouling. For that purpose, we need a simple and generic mathematical model able to predict the fouling dynamics. A generic model is a model that can reproduce as well as possible a quite large number of different situations in the field of membrane filtration for this prupose. Most existing fouling models are complicated: ei- ther they have many parameters, or their validity range is reduced since they are only valid [SOURCE: Page_51]

### 1.5. Modeling and control of fouling in membrane filtration systems                       35

under many assumptions, which limit their usefulness [Di Bella et al. 2008], [Janus 2014]. It is why some authors proposed simple models that can be suitable for control purposes. For example, [Benyahia et al. 2013] developed a fouling model which is mathematically simple to be used for the control and optimization of filtration systems. Several control strategies have been developed and applied to membrane filtration pro- cesses in order to improve their performances, such as for example [Smith et al. 2006] where it has been developed a control strategy that monitors the evolution of the trans- membrane pressure (TMP) over time and starts a 15-second backwash cycle when the TMP exceeds a given set point. Another strategy developed by [Robles et al. 2014] al- lows to optimize the backwash frequencies based on control of the permeability cal- culated from the online measurements of TMP and permeate flow. Moreover, strate- gies for optimal control of membrane processes have been developed preliminary by [Cogan & Chellam 2014], [Cogan et al. 2016], where a prediction of optimal switching times between filtration and backwash periods that maximizes net water production over a fixed operating time for membrane filtration process is obtained. Then comes the works of the thesis of [Kalboussi 2018], which has revisited the latter studies and developed a muich more generic optimal control for filtration/backwash sequences to maximize the produc- tion of permeate from a filtration system operating at constant TMP. Our approach is an extension of the works of [Kalboussi 2018], as we consider that its more appropriate to have the constant permeate flow with a variable TMP in a filtration system, when it comes to filtering wastewater, than to consider a constant TMP with variable permeate flow. [SOURCE: Page_52]

1.5.1   Optimal control In recent years, the optimal control theory has come out as a very interesting tool for optimizing the operation of membrane filtration systems. The aim of optimal control is to find the optimal control actions, that minimizes (or maximizes) an integral performance criterion related to the state of a dynamic system (with constraints if required) and doing so within a fixed time period or with a free terminal time [Harmand et al. 2019]. In principle„ an optimal control problem is essentially defined by three components: [SOURCE: Page_52]

• The process model: it defines the inputs, outputs and states and describes the dy- namics of the real system. [SOURCE: Page_52]

• The objective function (or optimization criteria): it expresses mathematically the costs to be minimized or the benefits to be maximized. [SOURCE: Page_52]

• The constraints: these denote the set of allowable values for the state or the control. [SOURCE: Page_52]

In this work, we used the Pontryagin Maximum Principle (PMP) to derive necessary op- timality conditions, because it is an important tool in the theory of optimal control. With the application of the PMP, we can exhaustively characterize the properties of optimal con- trol, understand all the subtility of its ‘synthesis’ and and even sometimes, express the optimal strategy in terms of state feedback. This principle, formulated by L. S. Pontrya- gin [Pontryagin 2018], gives necessary conditions of optimality and allows the determina- tion of optimal trajectories when they exist. This principle is available in several versions [SOURCE: Page_52]

36                        Chapter 1. Modeling and control of a WRRF for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_53]

derived from its initial version depending on terminal conditions and other possible con- straints. Indeed, the statement of its equations vary according to the objective and the constraints of the problem and in particular whether the final time is fixed or not in the cost function. The general statement of the Pontryagin Maximum Principle without constraints is recalled in the following Section 1.5.2. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Let us formulate the following remarks: [SOURCE: Page_54]

Chapter 2. From WWTP to WRRF : the necessity of more flexibility to adapt the 40                                     quality of treated waters to new usages [SOURCE: Page_57]

This chapter is being submitted in an international journal. It presents the main results of the manuscript related to the flexibility of treatment systems for REUSE in coupling the model of the platform with a model of plant growth to be used for the computation of optimal output nitrogen concentration to be delivered to the plant. The first step consists in a numerical investigation of the platform for analyzing the steady states that can be reached by manipulating the various controllers. Using these results, we propose an open-loop con- trol strategy to deliver the appropriate output nitrogen concentration depending on both the input effluent characteristics and the available controls. This strategy is satisfying as long as input effluent characteristic are known and constant. However, because the system operates in the presence of disturbances, and depending on the objective pursued, we propose two industrial-based closed-loop control strategies to automatically find the best system config- uration and controls with respect to the required output nitrogen concentration. These two strategies are evaluated in the presence of disturbances and uncertainty. [SOURCE: Page_57]
