### 2.1. Introduction                                                                             41

2.1    Introduction [SOURCE: Page_58]

The Mediterranean Rim is a hotspot within a context where current changes (population increase, global warming, increase of food demand, decline in biodiversity, etc.) lead to the necessity to priorize the withdraw of surface waters of good quality for human consump- tion. While human activities have increasing bad consequences on water quality - which are amplified by global changes - alternative water resources need to be found, agriculture being the largest consumer with 70 % of water consumption [Boretti & Rosa 2019], [Abbott et al. 2019], [Ouali et al. 2018]. To tackle this challenge, we must change the paradigm, turning from promoting maximum treatment of wastewater before disposal in the environment to considering wastewater as a water containing nutrients whose treatment has to be tailored according to usages [Ait-Mouheb et al. 2018], [Villarín & Merel 2020]. Indeed, recovering nutrients from wastewater is an important challenge of circular economy [Robles et al. 2020]. To do so, different systems exist depending on the desired goal. Some WWTP - rather called WRRF for water resources recovery factory in this context - are designed so as to recover energy [Cornel et al. 2011][Wang et al. 2020], while others optimize nutrient recovery [Nowak et al. 2004][Mo & Zhang 2013]. What about keeping these nutrients in the water at a level optimized for specific usages and promote fertigation practices? This would require more ‘flexible treatment systems’, as opposed to the actual ones that are usually only able to work in a static treatment mode both in industry or in urban areas whatever they allow nutrient recovering, or not [Hansen et al. 2016], [Murray & Ray 2010], [Vergine et al. 2017]. Actual WWTP operate in such a way the quality of the output effluent is as constant as possible to comply with normative constraints while the system in itself may be highly dynamical to ‘reject input disturbances’ (since the input flow and pollutant concentrations varies over time). Although not well defined in the specific context of REUSE - and it is not the objective of the present paper to do it - ‘flexibility’ reflects here the ‘robust’ ability of a treatment system to be dynamically controlled in such a way the quality of treated waters may be tailored depending on the required quality for its effective reuse. In other terms, it reflects the fact that there exists several ‘ways’ for the system to deliver a given quality of water (we will come back on this conceptual view later on). Indeed, available culture and plant models highlight a fundamental property of almost all plants: their ‘optimal requirements’ in terms of water and nutrients highly vary with time and, very often, are decoupled one from the other. In addition, the different forms of nitrogen may be important from an agronomic point of view, ammonium being much less subject to leaching than nitrate while being less bio-accessible. In actual irrigation practices, at best, it is only the quantity of water which is optimized while nutrients are supplied once or twice during the growth cycle of the crop. Depending on weather conditions and actual practices, it may happen that part of these nutrients be lost by run off or infiltration possibly causing damage to water tables. [SOURCE: Page_58]

As a consequence, a flexible WRRF should exhibit the following main characteris- tics: [SOURCE: Page_58]

Chapter 2. From WWTP to WRRF : the necessity of more flexibility to adapt the 42                                     quality of treated waters to new usages [SOURCE: Page_59]

• It should be able to deliver a water in which the ratios between the different forms of nitrogen could be controlled; [SOURCE: Page_59]

• It should be able to follow setpoints in terms of water flows and quality correspond- ing to those that can be considered as optimal with respect to agronomic needs; [SOURCE: Page_59]

• It should be able to switch from one mode to the other - that is from a ‘complete treatment’, in which the water content must comply with normative constraints to a ‘partial or degraded treatment mode’ in which water quality is managed according to usage - in a reasonable time while internal/external disturbances be rejected; [SOURCE: Page_59]

• These three points being constrained i) by the necessity to manage both environmen- tal and sanitary risks and ii) treatment costs; [SOURCE: Page_59]

Wastewater treatment by activated sludge does not effectively promote nutrient and energy recovery. During this treatment, the carbon and nitrogen (nutrients) in the wastewater are converted into biomass, nitrogen gas and carbon dioxide, which means that a large part of the energy and nutrient content of the wastewater is dissipated to the detriment of a significant energy supply [Sills et al. 2016]. Conventional wastewater treatment is carried out by combining the stages of nitrification and denitrification in two separate tanks maintained in aerobic and anoxic conditions, respectively. The pre-denitrification and post-denitrification configurations are the most common [Barker & Dold 1995]. In the pre-denitrification configuration, the anoxic tank is placed upstream of the aerobic reactor: it receives the nitrate from the aerobic tank through a recirculation loop. In the post-denitrification configuration, the anoxic tank is placed downstream of the aerobic reactor but often requires the addition of external carbon for denitrification. To deal with the advantages of all configurations, possibly only punctually - and thus in avoiding their drawbacks, we have conceived a platform including all of them (nitrogen removal with pre/post-denitrification or no treatment at all depending on the position of a number of valves and pumps). [SOURCE: Page_59]

The use of membrane bioreactors (MBRs) for wastewater treatment has increased dramatically due to technological advancements and lower operating costs for membranes. The high effluent quality offered compared to conventional activated sludge systems makes the membranes particularly suitable for reuse [Judd 2008], mainly due to the complete retention of solids and thus its capacity to prevent pathogen diffusion. In addition, the presence of a membrane allows the solid retention time to be accurately controlled. This is why the last tank of our flexible system is a membrane bioreactor. As a whole, the platform thus involves three tanks, one anoxic and two aerobic, the last tank being considered as a perfect membrane bioreactor. [SOURCE: Page_59]

The simulation of such a platform is possible using the activated sludge models (ASM) which have succeeded in forming an industrial standard for modeling wastewater treatment processes. Since ASM1 ([Henze et al. 2000]), several models of activated sludge (ASM) and some extensions have been published. They fixed some weaknesses [SOURCE: Page_59]

### 2.1. Introduction                                                                          43

of the ASM$_1$ and included new processes. Nevertheless, ASM$_1$ remains the model most often cited in the literature although nitrification is modeled as a one-step process. For our purpose (we must be able to manage the different forms of nitrogen), the nitrification will be modeled here as a two-stage process as proposed in [Ostace et al. 2011]. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Controlling the flexible platform is challenging in itself. To do so, we take advan- tage of control actions available on the process, called ‘actuators’, allowing us to act on the variables of the process while the available ‘sensors’ inform us about the state of the process. In the literature, we find many examples and applications of control strategies involving a set of control variables and sensors: actuators include flow rates (input, recirculation or waste flows), aeration power, positions of valves while sensors include ‘environmental variables’ like pH, temperature, pressure, flows, dissolved oxygen or ‘process variables’ like biomass, COD or nutrient concentrations in more advanced configurations. The main process characteristics to be controlled include the oxygen concentrations in the aerobic tanks, the hydraulic as well as the solid retention times or still nutrient concentrations or suspended solids that must comply with normative constraints. In control engineering, inputs and outputs are classified into different classes. Inputs include actuators - that we can tune according to a given control objective - and disturbances - that are either measured or not and the consequences of which we must counterbalance, precisely in using actuators. Outputs are usually classified in two families depending whether they are measured (through sensors) or not. For instance, it may be interesting to control a concentration which is not directly measured. In such a case, we must use an ‘observer’ - also called a ‘software sensor’ to reconstruct/estimate the output using the available information - the model and the available online - or offline - sensors. As mentioned here above, most control strategies available in the literature aim at maintaining, at lowest possible cost, the main process variables within ranges specified by normative constraints. Some works apply control strategies in sludge treatment as in [Barbu et al. 2018], but most of the articles do so in secondary treatment. Some of them aim to improve controlling the dissolved oxygen concentration (SO ) in aerobic reactors, by manipulating the oxygen transfer coefficient (KL a), using different control techniques [Revollar et al. 2018], [Santién et al. 2019]. Improving the setpoint regulation of (SO ) in the nitrification process has also been the goal of many papers, such as [Błaszkiewicz et al. 2014], [Santin et al. 2015]. Another variable that can be manipulated in biological treatment is the internal recirculation rate. Some control strategies allow to maintain this recirculation flowrate fixed, while others, like in [Santín et al. 2016] propose to consider it as the manipulated variable to maintain constant the nitrate setpoint (SN O$_3$ ) in the last anoxic reactor. It should be noticed here that the IWA is at the origin of a number of control benchmarking activities making intensive use of IWA models of activated sludge treatment systems and has contributed a lot to the development of this domain [Alex et al. 2018]. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Here, the challenge is to make extensive use of simulations, using the available models of the activated sludge systems, to propose a simulator for a flexible platform. Then, evaluating on this platform new control strategies able to manage the system such [SOURCE: Page_60]

Chapter 2. From WWTP to WRRF : the necessity of more flexibility to adapt the 44                                     quality of treated waters to new usages [SOURCE: Page_61]

that to adapt the quality of treated water to agronomic needs. In other words, as opposed to most available works of the literature, the control system will not be used to maintain the outputs at constant levels fixed by regulation constraints but rather to use the available control to act on the process in such a way the most important outputs, from a reuse viewpoint, follow the required setpoints. You still have to be able to determine what these setpoints should be, and notably, with respect to which objectives the control system has to be designed for. To do so, in parallel to this work, we used plant growth models and solve optimal control problems the results of which are candidate setpoints in terms of water quantity and quality over time. [SOURCE: Page_61]

The present paper is organized as follows. In the first section, the flexible platform and its model are presented, in checking the stoichiometric discontinuities for the model used is then given. In second section, the functioning modes are characterized with respect to the available actuators (valves and pumps): in particular the steady states the system can attain are computed via a numerical approach, in which an open loop control strategy has been proposed. In third section, a control system is proposed based on the implementation of simple robust industrial controllers in cascade. These controllers are then evaluated in fourth section using setpoints generated by means of plant models and corresponding to the optimal requirements of plants before some conclusions are drawn. [SOURCE: Page_61]

2.2     Materials and methods 2.2.1    A flexible platform [SOURCE: Page_61]

The flexible WRRF platform considered here has been developed within the framework of the European project ‘Control^4Reuse’ (cf. control^4reuse.net). The idea of the design was to build a configuration that contains the most important number of control degrees to maximize its flexibility: the platform proposes a mix between several configurations (pre- denitrification, post-denitrification and step-feed) using three valves, allowing the plant to operate in different modes (‘Reuse’, also called ‘degraded’ here, or ‘conventional treat- ment’ mode). [SOURCE: Page_61]

2.2.2    System description and design [SOURCE: Page_61]

The WRRF is made of three reactors in series: an anoxic reactor (R$_1$) of volume 4000 m^3 , and an aerobic reactor (R$_2$) of volume 6000 m^3 followed by an aerobic membrane biore- actor (R$_3$) of volume 3000 m^3 ; all reactors are completely mixed (cf 2.1), the membrane is ideal (perfect separation of soluble and particular matters) and thus no fouling phenomena are taken into account. The WRRF contains three valves (named (A), (B) and (C)) that have two positions. Referring to Figure 2.1, we adopt the following conventions: [SOURCE: Page_61]

• if V alve(A) = 1 (resp. V alve(A) = 0) −→ QB = Qin and QC = 0 (resp. QB = 0 and QC = Qin ); [SOURCE: Page_61]

### 2.2. Materials and methods                                                                 45

• if V alve(B) = 1 (resp. V alve(B) = 0) −→ QD = QB and QC = 0 (resp. QD = 0 and QC = QB ); • if V alve(C) = 1 (resp. V alve(C) = 0) −→ QE = QRAS and QF = 0 (resp. QE = 0 and QF = QRAS ). The WRRF has been designed for an average input flow of 24000 m^3 /d. The platform can be operated as a one- (only tank R$_3$), two- (tanks R$_2$ and R$_3$) or three- (tank R$_1$, R$_2$ and R$_3$) stage process by adjusting input flow rate (Qin ), step feeding (by-pass) and return activated sludge locations depending on the positions of the valves. [SOURCE: Page_62]

Figure 2.1: Hydraulic process scheme of the proposed flexible platform [SOURCE: Page_62]

The configuration in which the external recirculation (QRAS ) from R$_3$ to the anoxic tank R$_1$ with V alve(C) = 0 and the internal recirculation (Qint ) are nonzero allows to eliminate nitrogen by denitrification of recycled nitrate (corresponding to a pre- denitrification system). In such a configuration, the denitrification can be controlled by the recirculation rate. By feeding the aerobic tank R$_2$ with the input flow (V alve(A) = 1 and V alve(B) = 0), the denitrification takes place after the nitrification (corresponding to a post-denitrification system if V alve(C) = 0). In this configuration, the denitrification may become limiting because the carbon source may have been consumed in R$_2$ and may not be available anymore as an energy source. When the input flow is directed towards the MBR reactor R$_3$ (both V alves(A) and (B) equal 1), the nitrification can be controlled by the sludge retention time (SRT) and the dissolved oxygen concentration (DO). [SOURCE: Page_62]

Depending on valve’s position, there are 6 specific configurations of interest for the proposed flexible WRRF: they are reported in Table 2.1. There are many other configura- tions but they are not of interest: for instance, notice that when V alve(A) = 0, the position of the V alve(B) does not matter. [SOURCE: Page_62]

Chapter 2. From WWTP to WRRF : the necessity of more flexibility to adapt the 46                                     quality of treated waters to new usages [SOURCE: Page_63]

V alve(A)     V alve(B)     V alve(C)     Description 0             0             0             Configuration C$_1$ 0             0             1             Configuration C$_2$ 1             0             0             Configuration C$_3$ 1             0             1             Configuration C$_4$ 1             1             0             Configuration C$_5$ 1             1             1             Configuration C$_6$ [SOURCE: Page_63]

Table 2.1: Valve position for the 6 configurations of interest [SOURCE: Page_63]

The configuration C$_1$ (read V alve(A) = 0, V alve(B) = 0, and V alve(C) = 0 in Table 2.1) may be considered to be equivalent to the conventional activated sludge process which comprises a series of completely mixed reactors with a recirculation. [SOURCE: Page_63]

In the configuration C$_3$ (V alve(A) = 1, V alve(B) = 0 and V alve(C) = 0) the process will behave as a post-denitrification system without any carbon source addition: denitrification rate is expected to be low. If the hydraulic retention time (HRT) is large enough, the configuration may exhibit high rate nitrification as long as there is enough oxygen in R$_2$. [SOURCE: Page_63]

In the configuration C$_5$ (V alve(A) = 1, V alve(B) = 1, V alve(C) = 0), the nitrifi- cation will be limited due to a short HRT. The three remaining configurations are different from the first three by the position of V alve(C): this will mostly affect the performances of nitrification and denitrification depending on the quantity of nitrate which is injected back to R$_1$. A number of additional hypotheses have been introduced: [SOURCE: Page_63]

• It is supposed that there is no nitrate at the process input and it is all under ammoni- acal form and as proposed by IWA, the total nitrogen content can be computed using the formula N T K = 32 NN H$_4$ + 13 Norganic ; [SOURCE: Page_63]

• The maximum recirculation rate of the mixed liquor Qint is 100 % compared to the input flow (Qint = 24000 m^3 /d); [SOURCE: Page_63]

• The maximum sludge recirculation rate QRAS is 500 % compared to the flow the input flow (QRAS = 120000 m^3 /d); [SOURCE: Page_63]

• As for BSM1, the WRRF is designed for a constant influent dry-weather flow rate of 24000 m3 .d−1 and a constant biodegradable COD in the influent of 350 g.m−3 . When dynamical inputs are used, their average values are given in Table 2.2; The volumes of tanks are chosen such that the minimum possible HRT equals 3 hours. [SOURCE: Page_63]

### 2.2. Materials and methods                                                                   47

2.2.3   Model description The WRRF platform is modelled according to the flow scheme represented in Fig.2.1. The biological reactions inside each reactor are modeled using the ASM$_1$-2ND model [Ostace et al. 2011]. As already mentioned, it differs from the original ASM$_1$ [Henze et al. 2000] by considering a two-step reaction for the nitrification process realized by two groups of autotrophic bacteries, namely the AOB and the NOB for ammonium- oxidizing and nitrite oxidizing bacteries and named in the model XAOB and XN OB , re- spectively. The aerobic growth rates of the AOB and NOB (ρ5 and ρ6 in Table 2.4) are given by: [SOURCE: Page_64]

SN H       SO ρAOB = µAOB                           XAOB                          (2.1) KN H + SN H KOA + SO SN O$_2$        SO        KN H ρN OB = µN OB                                       XN OB                   (2.2) KN O$_2$ + SN O$_2$ KOA + SO SN H + KN H Equation (2.1) describes the first step of nitrification (partial nitrification): when the am- monium is oxidized into nitrite, both ammonium (SN H ) and dissolved oxygen (SO ) act as limiting factors, while in Eq. (2.2) by using a ‘switching function’, the ammonium (SN H ) behaves as an inhibitor for the second oxidation step of nitrite (SN O$_2$ ) into nitrate(SN O$_3$ ). For the denitrification, the facultative heterotrophic biomass can remove organic carbon through anoxic respiration by using nitrite or nitrate, which serve as electron acceptors in the absence of dissolved oxygen and transform them into dinitrogen. For the denitrification, the reaction rates (ρ2 and ρ3 in Table 2.4) are given by the following expressions: [SOURCE: Page_64]

Ss      KOH       SN O$_2$        SN H ρBHN O$_2$ = µH ηN O$_2$                                              XBH                  (2.3) Ks + Ss KOH + SO KN O + SN O$_2$ KN H + SN H Ss      KOH       SN O$_3$        SN H ρBHN O$_3$ = µH ηN O$_3$                                              XBH                  (2.4) Ks + Ss KOH + SO KN O + SN O$_3$ KN H + SN H The list of model parameters as well as the kinetics/stoichiometric coefficients values are reported in Table 2.12. The complete WRRF model is defined by the following equations for the soluble Si,R and particulate Xi,R components in all reactors where i stands for the component (i = 1, 2, ..., 16) (cf. 2.2.4    Stoichiometric discontinuities checking In order to close the COD and nitrogen balance, the total COD and N reacting under pro- cess conditions must match the total COD and N consumption. Balance equations form the basis of any model description. These equations describe the way the concentrations vary in a reactor over the time as the result of chemical and biological conversions and of trans- port processes. ### 2.2. Materials and methods                                                                49

some typing errors and inconsistencies in their stoichiometric matrix. Before using it, we should check the continuity of the stoichiometric and the consistency of the kinetic rate expressions ([Hauduc 2011]). This verification does not intend to address model structure problems linked either to modelling concepts or to simplifications used in the ASM$_1$-2ND model. Rather, the approach checks and corrects the COD and nitrogen mass balances to ensure the continuity and conservation of the chemical elements (C, N, O). Generally, each component is represented by a state variable, which is typically expressed in terms of COD equivalents, elements (e.g. N) or charge. The Gujer matrix (Table. 2.3, 2.4 contains both the stoichiometric coefficients for each state variable and the associated kinetics for each state variable. Browsing the column associated with a given variable allows us    to see  in which conversions it is involved. By multiplying the stoichiometric coefficients vj,i with       their respective rate equations, one gets the total conversion equation for each compound. To check continuity, the composition matrix is formed in terms of conserved balances (cf. Table 2.6), that is terms of COD, N and charge. In the stoichiometric matrix, the biomass is expressed in terms of COD, but it contain also some nitrogen: this is taken into account in the composition matrix. The continuity is checked by multiplying the composition ma- trix (Table. 2.6) and the stoichiometry matrix (Table. 2.5), that contain all the conserved balances. The multiplication of the two matrices leads to zero when the balance is closed. P11 j=1 vj,i ci,comp = 0 with     comp = COD, N, charge. In particuler, the kinetic rate expression to model nutrient (ammonia) limitation in the heterotrophic growth process was missed in the original model, which could in- duce negative ammonia concentration values. To correct this error, a Monod kinetics SN H KN H,H SN H was added in the three first processes. Such a checking has been realized by ([Petersen et al. 2001]) ([Rieger et al. 2012]) for the ASM$_1$. Notice that since the stoi- chiometric matrix published in ([Ostace et al. 2011]) for the ASM$_1$-2ND model contained many typing errors, the whole corrected stoichiometric matrix for the ASM$_1$-2ND model has been rewritten (cf. Table. 2.3) In particuler, the kinetic rate expression to model nutrient (ammonia) limitation in the heterotrophic growth process was missed in the original model, which could in- duce negative ammonia concentration values. 2.3       Results 2.3.1     Numerical analysis of the platform at steady state 2.3.1.1    Numerical procedure WRRF simulations are carried out in the same way as described in the BSM1, i.e. initial conditions correspond to the simulations of the system under constant inputs by using the values reported in Table 2.2 over 300 days. This time period was found sufficient to reach a pseudo-steady-state for all states in the system. Concerning the dynamical simulations of the system (when it is subject to time-varying inputs), the input data set for the influ- ent dynamic concentrations corresponds to that one given in the BSM1LT in dry weather condition [BSM ]: these data, from t = 0 to t = 300, are used for dynamic simulations (cf. Figures 2.2 and 2.3). Consider the platform represented in Figure 2.1, where the configurations of interest related to the specific valve positions are reported in Table 2.1. The first analysis of this platform consists in characterizing the steady-states it is possible to attain in playing with the different valves of the system. To do so, a total of NSS = NV alve × NkLa × NQ sim- ulations (cf. Figure 2.4) were performed using static influent values reported in Table 2.2, for all design configurations (i.e., each given position of valves over the 6 configurations [SOURCE: Page_68]

of interest) and for a grid of possible control values acting on the process through KL a^1 , KL a^2 , QRAS , QW AS and Qint (see Figure 2.4). To investigate the attainable steady states, a simulation loop was launched for each combination of control variables. The discretization of control degree values was uniform. The steady states set formed by the NSS steady states obtained (all state variables of the effluent - the values of the variables in the last aerobic compartment together with the corresponding process configuration and the associated control variables (reported in Table 2.7)) were saved in a steady-state matrix, which has (30240 × 24) elements consisting of two sub-matrices (cf. Table. 2.8) for the variables (30240 × 16) and the control values (30240 × 8), respectively. [SOURCE: Page_73]

Some simulations are plotted in 2.5 to illustrate the range of steady states it is possible to attain in playing with the manipulated variables (see appendix C). Notice that using a given combination of the controls makes it possible to favor a given form of nitrogen with respect to others. A particularly interesting feature of the exercise is that playing both with the configuration of the system and with control actions makes is possible to achieve more or less the same target, leading to an important characteristic of the flexibility concept, which opens the route to important research about the automatic operation of such a system, notably in the area of reconfiguration control systems [Aichouche et al. 2020]. [SOURCE: Page_73]

Chapter 2. From WWTP to WRRF : the necessity of more flexibility to adapt the 58                                     quality of treated waters to new usages [SOURCE: Page_75]

the valves and the control values) to obtain, at steady state, these required output charac- teristics. Indeed, assume the control objective is to follow a setpoint given as a piecewise constant nitrate concentration. Further assume the static inputs of the process are known and ‘close’ to those with which the numerous simulations realized in the previous section have been realized (cf. Table 2.2). An algorithm can then browse the database composed of all situations simulated - and depending on the inputs and on the required nitrate concen- tration - find the one which is the closest, and finally adopt the corresponding configuration while the controls are set to those values found in the database. In other words, the open- loop control strategy simply consists in applying the corresponding configuration of valves and control inputs to the process for it to deliver the appropriate output nitrate concentra- tion. In order to illustrate such a flexibility concept, this open-loop control strategy is tested in simulation by using the algorithm [1] reported hereafter with the piecewise output con- centration setpoint plotted in Figures 2.6 2.7 for both constant and then dynamical inputs (dashed line), respectively. For the static case, we assume the static inputs are exactly those given in Table 2.2. For the dynamic case, the dynamical input data used correspond to in- puts of the dry weather conditions used in the BSM1LT [BSM ] from t = 0 to t = 300 days. We consider the piecewise constant setpoint for the nitrate plotted in Figure (2.6). For each value of the setpoint, the configuration - out of the six reported in Table 2.1 - and repre- sented by the succession of colors - is identified as the one allowing to obtain the closest nitrate value saved during the open loop simulations. Table 2.9 reports the corresponding configuration and control values that must be applied. ∗ Algorithm 1 Search SN O$_3$ in data.SN O$_3$ i←0 while data.SN O$_3$ [i] 6= SN ∗ O$_3$ do i ← i+1 ∗ if data.SN O$_3$ [i] is equal to SN O$_3$ then kL a^1 ← data.kL a^1 [i] kL a^2 ← data.kL a^2 [i] VA ← data.VA [i] VB ← data.VB [i] VC ← data.VC [i] QW AS ← data.QW AS [i] QRAS ← data.QRAS [i] Qint ← data.Qint [i] ∗ else (SN O$_3$ is not fined) [SOURCE: Page_75]

We can notice that the approach is particularly efficient: the process delivers a value for the output nitrate concentration which is very close to the required setpoint whatever the input are constant (cf Figure (2.6) or dynamic (cf Figure 2.6). However, it should be noticed that such an approach makes important hypotheses, in particular: [SOURCE: Page_75]

• The inputs are assumed to be constant and perfectly known; [SOURCE: Page_75]

• The model is assumed to be noise free and its parameters perfectly known in order to build the database; [SOURCE: Page_77]

• At present time, this open loop approach is not optimized at all. In particular, taking advantage of the flexibility of the platform, it may happen that two different config- urations allow the same output to be obtained: in such a case it could be interesting to consider other criteria such as the one requiring the less energy or still the one minimizing sludge production. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Otherwise said, the open-loop control strategy requires a very high quantity of information which is usually unavailable in a real case: the inputs are usually not well characterized while the system is subject to uncertainty or disturbances and the measurements subject to noise. It is why a closed-loop approach should be preferred. [SOURCE: Page_77]

2.3.2     Dynamical analysis 2.3.2.1   Two industrial strategies for the closed-loop operation and control of the WRRF platform [SOURCE: Page_77]

The Proportional-Integral PI control remains one of the the most widely used controller in industry due to its simplicity and ease of tuning. For a PI controller, the error signal, e(t) between the setpoint and actual process output is used to generate the proportional (P) and integral(I) control actions with the resulting signals weighted and summed to form the con- trol signal u(t) to be applied to the plant ([Bennett 1993]). The mathematical expression of the PI controller is recalled in (2.11) where KP and KI are the proportional and integral gains of the controller: Z t u(t) = Kp e(t) + Ki          e(τ ) dτ                    (2.11) 0 [SOURCE: Page_77]

A saturation may be added as a guarantee that the desired setpoint cannot exceed the maximum capacity of the manipulated variables. This can cause the phenomenon of windup, which reflects the fact that the error between the setpoint and the measurement is non-zero and remains of the same sign. However, the simulation results showed here that [SOURCE: Page_77]

### 2.3. Results                                                                                61

such a system was not necessary. In this work, two types of control systems are proposed depending on the objective pursued: [SOURCE: Page_78]

1. The first control strategy focuses on the development of a system allowing a trajec- tory tracking of the effluent nitrate concentration. The ‘advanced industrial control’ consists in a cascade of PI controllers represented in Figure 2.8 (or in the Figure. 2.9 for more illustration): the kL a to be applied to aerobic reactors is the output of a PI controlling the dissolved oxygen concentration in the reactor such that a setpoint in SN H$_4$ to be attained, this SN H$_4$ setpoint being itself the output of a controller hav- ing in its input the SN O$_3$ setpoint. However, the nitrate concentration being affected by the denitrification, this strategy necessitates the addition of three additional con- trollers allowing the adjustment of the internal and the external recirculation loops through Qint , QRAS and QW AS , respectively, where each PI controls the SN O$_3$ in the last MBR reactor. These controllers allow the control of the SRT of the system. Notice that each control acts as a kind of disturbance for the others but since the system is stable, it does not affect the global performances of the system as it will be seen in the results. In addition, the configuration is very robust since, in case of a loop failure, the others continue to regulate the nitrate concentration. The valve position control is also acting as a disturbance for the system. It can be qualified of an industrial controller since it uses only two measurements that are the ammo- nium and nitrate concentrations in the lats basin (SN O$_3$ being precisely the variable to be controlled in this first control strategy). The 6 controller parameters KP (i) and Ki (i), i = 1..3 were tuned by trial and error using dynamical input and setpoint data. In addition, the control QW AS was saturated: 0m^3 /d ≤ QW AS ≤ 1300m^3 /d. [SOURCE: Page_78]

2. The second control strategy represented in Figure 2.10 (or in the Figure. 2.11 for more illustration) consists in controlling a ratio between the nitrate and ammonia concentrations SN O$_3$ /SN H$_4$ . Again a combination of PI controllers is proposed. The closed-loop control configuration consists of 4 independent PI controllers, all having as single input the actual ratio SN O$_3$ /SN H$_4$ , which is supposed to be mea- sured. The 4 actuators of the 4 PI controllers are i) KL a (the same aeration control is applied to avoid partial nitrification) to control the oxygen concentration SO in the last reactor and ii) the three recirculation and waste sludge pumps Qint , QRAS and QW AS . Saturation was considered to limit kL a: 0 ≤ kL a ≤ 300d−1 . The returned activated sludge flow rate QRAS limits are 0 ≤ QRAS ≤ 5Qin and 0 ≤ Qint ≤ Qin . At first guess, gains KP (i) and KI (i), i = 1..4, are initially tuned using the Matlab’s linearizing tool before being optimized to improve their performance with a trial and error approach. Again, notice that this approach is particularly robust with respect to actuator failure since each controller can compensate for problems arising on other control loops. [SOURCE: Page_78]

for irrigation. The weighted average values of effluent concentrations over the evaluation periods in complete treatment mode should be within the limits shown in Table 2.2. Total nitrogen (Ntot) is calculated as the sum of SN O$_3$ , SN O$_2$ , SN H and SN D . Since we consider perfect separation, particulate nitrogen could not be present in the effluent, and the total chemical oxygen demand CODtot equals the soluble chemical oxygen demand CODS = SI + SS . [SOURCE: Page_81]

The result of this strategy of control for complete treatment mode is given in Figure 2.13 where Ntot in the output is plotted and in Figure 2.14 where the output BOD$_5$ and CODtot concentrations are plotted. The corresponding controllers are reported in Figure 2.15. It can be observed that, although considering highly disturbed inputs, this strategy was able to keep the effluent under the discharge standards (Table. 2.10) in terms of total nitrogen Ntot , N H$_4$ , CODtot and BOD$_5$ . 2.3.3   Proof of concept: ‘irrigation’ or ‘degraded mode’ - closed-loop control performances with a piecewise constant setpoint In the previous section, it was demonstrated that the platform is able to deliver waters which the quality complies with normative constraints - operation mode to be used during periods where no water is needed for irrigation. In this section, we show how the proposed strategies behave when there is a need for irrigation. The WRRF was simulated to first perform i) nitrate control trajectory tracking by using the first closed-loop strategy (2.9 and 2.8) under constant input conditions (no input disturbance) and then under dynamical input conditions. Secondly ii) to perform nitrate to ammonium nitrogen ratio control trajectory tracking closed-loop strategy (2.10 and 2.11) under dynamical input condition. [SOURCE: Page_83]

1. For the ‘degraded mode’ (we adapt the output nitrate concentration to requirements), when constant inputs are used (cf. Table 2.2), the trajectory tracking for the output nitrate concentrations are plotted in Figures 2.16-2.20. As it may be seen, the results are very good. The nitrate concentration is perfectly tracked while all the inputs remain in their admissible ranges and never saturate. The main difference with the results obtained in open-loop is that here the input concentrations are unknown [SOURCE: Page_83]

2. In the second sets of simulations, dynamical input disturbances are used. The results are plotted in Figures 2.21-2.25. Again, the nitrate output concentration follows very well the setpoint and the actuators remain within their respective functioning ranges and never saturate (except for kL a^1,2 but very punctually) [SOURCE: Page_83]

3. Then, the nitrate to ammonium nitrogen ratio control is tested. Very good results (cf. Figures 2.26-2.27) are obtained in the presence of dynamical input disturbances (data obtained with constant inputs are not shown). The nitrate to ammonium nitro- gen ratio follows quite well the setpoint while the control remain within admissible values [SOURCE: Page_83]

2.3.4     Application to fertigation : coupling treatment and irrigation/culture models Setpoint generation In the previous section, we presented the performance results of the two proposed control strategies depending on the objective pursued using piecewise con- stant set-points. The objective of the present section is to show how the WRRF, a kind of ‘treatment plant of the future’ can be managed to deliver water quantity and quality (in terms of nitrogen concentrations) adapted to cultures/plants. Thus we concentrate on the application of the first control strategy. To do so, it is necessary to generate the water and nitrogen set-points to be followed and that could be considered as the ‘optimal ones’, in a sense to be specified. To generate these set-points, we propose to use available crop models and solve an optimal control problem. There are a very large number of such models in the literature (cf. for instance the state of the art proposed by [Jones et al. 2017]). Most of them, for not saying all, only include water needs (no consideration of nitrogen) and are by far too complicated to be used in optimization approaches. Recently, a crop model ([Pelak et al. 2017]) has been proposed following what we could call a systemic modelling approach from an automatic control viewpoint. It appears to be sufficiently simple for our purpose. The idea we followed is to apply to such a model optimal control techniques to find the ‘best’ water and nitrogen trajectories as setpoint for our work. Since it is not the objective of the present paper to work on the optimal control of plant growth, we do not further present the approach but rather use the generated setpoint as presented in the following figures (optimal total nitrogen requirements are plotted in dashed black line). In addition, since nitrogen is brought to the plant by water, it is assumed there is more water than necessary and we essentially concentrate on nitrogen content. It is then supposed that the optimal needs in term of water can be perfectly followed. Thus, we only use the total nitrogen requirement trajectory for the nitrate setpoint. Since we work with a membrane system, note that the nitrogen contained in the biomass is not released in the effluent and total nitrogen in the output cannot account on this part. It will be confirmed by the simula- tions but it is almost equivalent to control output nitrate concentration than controlling the total output nitrogen content of the effluent. [SOURCE: Page_89]

2.3.4.1    Performance indexes It should be noticed also that we do not know a priori which of the 6 possible configurations reported in Table 2.1 is the most appropriate for the system to better track the optimal ni- trogen trajectory. Thus, all six configurations were simulated first in the absence (unknown constant inputs) and then in the presence of disturbances (in the presence of dynamic in- puts, cf. Figures 2.28 and Figures 2.29, respectively). In order to compare the different configurations, important performance indexes such as the total amount of nitrogen provided to the crop through the effluent (both using static (AN T S) and dynamic influent (AN T D)) were computed for each configuration as : Z 139 T otalAmountof N itrogen =              Ntot Qef dt              (2.12) 0 [SOURCE: Page_89]

Where Ntot is the concentration of total nitrogen, Qef is the effluent. [SOURCE: Page_89]

The pumping en- ergy cost (P E)[kWh d−1 ] is calculated using the product of the flow rate with the pumping economy coefficient P F (kWh m−3 ) as: i=N          Z tsimul 1    X PE =                P Fi              Qi (t)dt           (2.13) tsimul i=1           0 [SOURCE: Page_90]

Where N=3, Q$_1$ (t)=Qint , Q$_2$ (t)=QRAS and Q$_3$ (t)=QW AS . The (P E) is calculated with- out taking into account the energy for pumping the effluent by the membrane. The pumping economy coefficients are taken from the BSM$_1$: Z 139 1 PE =                  (0.004Qint (t) + 0.008QRAS (t) + 0.05QW AS (t))dt         (2.14) 139    0 [SOURCE: Page_90]

P E is calculated for all considered configurations and for the complete treatment mode. Energy demand for aeration (AE)[kWh d−1 ] cost is calculated with Equation 2.15, which relates AE to the oxygen mass transfer coefficient kL a in each aerated reactor: Z 139 1 AE =                          VR$_2$ KL a^1 + VR$_3$ KL a^2 dt           (2.15) 139 × 103    0 [SOURCE: Page_90]

where VR$_2$ and VR$_3$ are the volumes of the second and final reactors respectively. We noted (P ES) for the pumping energy when using static influent, (P ED) when using dynamic in- fluent, as well as, (AES) and (AED) when using static and dynamic influent respectively. [SOURCE: Page_90]

1. Simulation results when the mean input total nitrogen concentration is 35g.N/m^3 Figures 2.28 show that as underlined above and whatever the configuration consid- ered i) the output nitrate concentration is very close to the total nitrogen concentra- tion, because the optimal amount of nitrogen demanded by the plant before t < 35 is always less than or equal to the amount of nitrogen in the inputs, ii) unless in the transient at the very beginning of the time period, there is almost no nitrite (which is toxic) in the effluent and iii) from about t = 35 there is obviously not enough total nitrogen in the input for the system to be able to perfectly track the output nitrogen setpoint. On overall, it can be seen that configuration C$_4$ seems to be the best to achieve our objectives: over the total period of time, it is the one which delivers the highest quantity of nitrogen (see. Table 2.11) . As expected, Table 2.11 shows that the degraded mode (for adapting the quality of wastewater treated to crops re- quirements) costs significantly less than treating using the complete mode operation. While the C$_4$ configuration was the best in terms of nitrogen supplied to plants, the configuration C$_2$ has the advantage of providing the best results in terms of costs highlighting a necessary compromise between energy used and savings in chemical fertilizer. [SOURCE: Page_90]

2.3.4.2   Limits of the approach [SOURCE: Page_92]

In the previous sections, it was supposed that the irrigation is not limited by the avail- able water. This means that the water flow produced by the treatment plant (which equals Qin − QW AS ) is greater than the water required by the culture. In other words, the WRRF produces more water than needed by the culture. If this water contains a high quantity of nitrogen, the water not used for irrigation cannot be released in the environment. In such a case it is necessary either to produce less water (which appears to be a complex problem [SOURCE: Page_92]

An alternative is to conceive systems where different qualities of wa- ters including the quality provided by the WRRF, can be mixed together. This approach yields complex, but very interesting optimal control problems that are actually not solved but that should be investigated in future work. [SOURCE: Page_93]

2.4    Conclusions The present study presented the modeling, the analysis and the control of a platform pro- posed for the recovery of nitrogen resources from treated wastewater. The flexibility of the platform was studied. A dynamical model was developed to be used for dynamic simula- tions under both static and dynamic conditions.Using intensive simulations at steady state, an open loop control strategy was proposed the limitations of which were highlighted. To overcome the weaknesses of this open-loop strategy, closed-loop approaches were devel- oped to allow the trajectory tracking of the nitrogen forms (nitrite or ratio between the nitrate and ammonia) in the effluent. The first strategy focuses on the control of the effluent nitrate concentration: we first applied it for the ‘complete treatment mode’ which should typically be used when reuse is not needed. To be more realistic, the simulations were performed assuming the presence of disturbances in the input (dynamic influent). The results showed that this strategy is able to keep the effluent under the discharge standards. Moreover, the same strategy was applied for nitrate control trajectory tracking for ‘irrigation’ or ‘degraded mode’ under two input conditions (static and dynamic). For both cases nitrate concentration perfectly tracked the [SOURCE: Page_93]

### 2.4. Conclusions                                                                             77

references while all the controllers remained in their admissible ranges without saturating. Depending on agronomic practices, a second control strategy was developed allowing the user to play with the different forms of nitrogen. Indeed, if nitrogen is mainly under the nitrate form, it is readily leachable into the soil, which is not suitable for some soils for which it may be desirable to prefer the ammonia form. Regarding these issues, the sec- ond strategy is developed to perform nitrate to ammonium nitrogen ratio control trajectory tracking. Again, very good results were obtained in the presence of dynamical input dis- turbances: the nitrate to ammonium ratio followed quite well the setpoint. Finally, a major original aspect of this work was the use the modeling and dynamic simulations for coupling the treatment mode with optimal references of plant/crops nitrogen requirement generated using a crop model. The approach was evaluated using the six possible configurations for the WRRF platform proposed both in the absence and in the presence of disturbances us- ing the nitrate control trajectory tracking. Among the different configurations, the results show that if the total nitrogen available in the influent is lower than that required by the crop, whatever the platform configuration used and the control strategy, it is never possible to achieve the required nitrogen levels. Moreover, thanks to its flexibility, some configu- rations do better than others under the same inputs both in view of the nitrogen quantity supplied to the soil/plants and energy costs. Finally, the results showed that modeling and control tools are able to provide significant contributions to the concept of flexibility, which appears central for conceiving future WRRF. [SOURCE: Page_94]

80      Chapter 3. Flexibility of Waste Resource Recovery Facilities for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_97]

This chapter was published : F. Aichouche, B. Benyahia, M. Héran and J. Harmand, "Flexibility of Waste Resource Recovery Facilities for water Reuse," 2020 European Control Conference (ECC), Saint Petersburg, Russia, 2020, DOI: 10.23919/ECC51009.2020.9143734. Flexibility is required for a more efficient management of water in order to deal with a multiple of (sometimes new) usages. When reusing treated wastewater for irrigation dur- ing periods of water stress, conserving some nutrients in treated water for plant needs - in ‘degrading the treatment degree’ - may present some advantages. On the contrary, when no water is needed for irrigation, the treatment plant must be able to realize a ‘full treatment’ (in eliminating the pollutants from wastewater and respect the limit imposed by discharge standards). This flexibility in the treatment ability requires a transition between these two operation modes to be minimized. The aim of this work is to study the concept of flexi- bility related to the capacity to adjust the water treatment degree - as quickly as possible - to changes in. This demand corresponds for instance to plant/crops requirements within the context of treated wastewater reuse while, in other periods, it corresponds to norma- tive constraints. The purpose of the proposed approach is to control the transition between these two operating modes by considering different scenarios. In this paper, we analyze a flexible platform and study different combinations of controls (valves positions, recircula- tion flows and sludge age) highlighting several functioning modes to be chosen depending on REUSE usages. [SOURCE: Page_97]

### 3.1. Abstract                                                                             81

3.1    Abstract In this paper, a new flexible wastewater treatment scheme is proposed to be used within the framework of wastewater reuse (REUSE). From a configuration where the water is treated to guarantee normative constraints, we propose a flexible configuration able to adapt, in minimal time, the quality and the quantity of water to usage. In other terms, the proposed scheme is able to treat water for purpose. Using model analysis and simulations, we show how a simulation platform may be used to demonstrate the flexible characteristics of the scheme, in particular when dealing with the decoupling of water fluxes and the different forms of nitrogen contents in the output treated water. [SOURCE: Page_98]

3.2    Introduction Actual wastewater treatment plants (WWTP) aim at treating wastewater in such a way they comply with normative constraints before discharged into environment (rivers lakes etc). However there is the need nowadays for a paradigm shift within the framework of circular economy: from a waste to be treated wastewater can be viewed as new resources to be processed for purpose. To do so, WWTP have to be operated with more ‘flexibility’. Although intensive attention has been given to WWTP, especially to biological ni- trogen and carbon removal processes [Kraume et al. 2005], a large number of alterna- tive configurations have been proposed [Hreiz et al. 2015] in order to improve the per- formance of the treatment either in terms of cost [Zarrad et al. 2004] or in terms of quality [van Kempen et al. 2005]. In any case, the configuration should contain an aerobic capac- ity to promote the nitrification while an anoxic zone must be used to promote denitrifica- tion. The two-steps nitrification-denitrification process revealed to be the most economical viable for the total elimination of nitrogen and carbon. Conventional WWT is accomplished by combining denitrification and nitrification steps requiring oxygen and organic carbon, respectively. The separation of conditions may be done in time or in space by imposing sequentially anoxic and aerobic conditions in a single reactor [Du Toit et al. 2007], or, by having a minimum of 2 reactors of which one is maintained anoxic and the other aerobic. The pre-denitrification and the post-denitrification configurations are the most widespread [Barker & Dold 1995]. In the pre-denitrification design, the anoxic tank is placed upstream of the aerobic reactor in order to use the car- bon present in the effluent for the denitrification. Therefore, nitrified liquor recycling to the anoxic reactor is needed to recirculate nitrate from the aerobic reactor. In the post- denitrification configuration, the anoxic tank is placed downstream of the aerobic reactor. This configuration allows a better nitrate removal efficiency than the pre-denitrification process. However, since carbon source is consumed in the aerated tank, it may become limiting in the anoxic reactor. Therefore, the addition of carbon source may be required in order to achieve an efficient denitrification. The flexibility concept in WWTP relates to its capacity to adjust - as quickly as pos- sible - to changes in demand, corresponding for instance to plant needs within the context of treated wastewater reuse. On one side, the flexible configuration allows to operate the [SOURCE: Page_98]

82      Chapter 3. Flexibility of Waste Resource Recovery Facilities for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_99]

WWTP in a mode to eliminate the components from wastewater or, on the other side, to keep nutrients or to decouple the water content from the water quantity (play with nutrient contents in the output effluent). In classical configurations, the nitrogen in ammoniacal form (N H$_4$+ ) is oxidized into nitrates (N O$_3$− ) and then reduced into nitrogen gas (N$_2$ ). In a flexible system, it is expected that the level of the different forms of nitrogen may be adjusted with respect to agriculture requirements. for instance, in some cases, the ammo- niacal form of nitrogen (N H$_4$+ ) will be favored while in others the nitrate form will be preferred. Moreover, keeping (N H$_4$+ ) requires lower energy consumption than when water is treated to provide nitrates (N O$_3$− ). Indeed, performing nitrification requires much more energy because nitrifying bacteria need more time to develop and thus induce a higher oxy- gen consumption. However,the ammoniacal nitrogen is not directly assimilable by plants: microorganisms in the soil are responsible for transforming (N H$_4$+ ) into (N O$_3$− ) that are more bioavailable. In the present paper, a new flexible configuration is suggested in the aim to control the transition between the conventional treatment (full treatment mode) and the adaptive treatment (reuse mode) by considering different scenarios. Our first objective is to analyze the best combination between valves positions, recirculation flows and sludge age for each operation mode, and to use this different degrees of freedom to minimize the time response to go from one mode to another one. When designed with the concern of recovering mate- rial and/or energy from a given effluent, a WWTP is renamed WRRF for Waste Resource Recovery Facilities. From now and upward, we will thus use WRRF instead of WWTP. [SOURCE: Page_99]

3.3     A flexible platform of WRRF [SOURCE: Page_99]

3.3.1   Platform description [SOURCE: Page_99]

The flexible platform configuration consists of an anoxic tank (V4 = 56 L) followed by aer- obic tank (V5 = 56L) and aerobic tank with membrane (MBR) (V6 = 40 L). The membrane is supposed to be ideal (perfect separation of soluble and particular matters) to promote sludge separation (fouling phenomena were not taken into account). The plant was de- signed to treat a flow Qin = 270 L/d. The platform (represented in Figure 3.1) can be operated as a one (only tank 6) - or two (tanks 5 and 6) - or three (Tank 4, 5 and 6) - stage process by adjusting influent feeding location (step feeding) and recirculation rates. Denitrification takes place in the anoxic tank whereas nitrification and carbon oxidation are accomplished in both aerobic and MBR tanks. Since nitrates are not present in the influent, they have to be brought into the anoxic reactor by internal recycling of the nitrified mixed liquor from the aerobic reactor when there is a need to reduce the effluent nitrate. This is done by tuning the external recirculation and playing with the position of the valve ‘C’. The waste activated sludge (WAS) flow rate in MBR tank is controlled to reach a specific sludge retention time corresponding to different operation modes (full / Adaptive). The dissolved oxygen (DO) concentration in the aerobic and MBR tank are maintained above 2.0 mg/L, while the DO level of the anoxic tank is supposed to be 0 mg/L. The constant influent characteristics are available in the table 3.1. [SOURCE: Page_99]

3.3.2   Model description The biological reactions inside each reactor in the flexible platform are modeled using ASM$_1$-2ND model [Ostace et al. 2011]. It is a modified version of Activated Sludge Model 1 (ASM$_1$) [Henze et al. 2000]. The main modification consists in the addition of a two-step process for nitrification and denitrification. While the ASM$_1$ model consider only complete nitrification of ammonia (N H$_4$+ ) to nitrate (N O$_3$− ) by autotrophic bacteria, followed by complete denitrification of nitrate to nitrogen gas, ASM$_1$-2ND uses a 2-steps nitrification model: the nitrification is carried out in two sequential steps, the first one describing the ammonium (N H$_4$+ ) oxidation to nitrite (N O$_2$− ) by Ammonia Oxidizing Bacteria (XAOB ) while the second one models the nitrite oxidation (N O$_2$− ) into nitrate (N O$_3$− ) by Nitrate Oxidizing Bacteria (XN OB ). The denitrification is performed following a parallel two- step scheme in which nitrate (N O$_3$− ) or nitrite (N O$_2$− ) is transformed into gas (N$_2$ ) by heterotrophic bacteria (XBH ).The processes describing the growth of aerobic bacteria are modeled using Monod kinetics and are presented in the equations (3.1) and (3.2). The aerobic growth kinetics of Ammonia Oxidizing Bacteria ρAOB (mg L−1 d−1 ) is mod- eled by: SN H          S$_0$ ρAOB = µAOB                             XAOB                    (3.1) KN H + SN H KOA + SO The aerobic growth kinetics of Nitrate Oxidizing Bacteria ρN OB (mg L−1 d−1 ) is modeled by: SN O$_2$           S$_0$         KN H ρN OB = µN OB                                          XN OB           (3.2) KN O$_2$ + SN O$_2$ KOA + SO SN H + KN H Where KOA ,KN O$_2$ and KN H are half-saturation rates of oxygen, nitrite and ammonium, respectively. µAOB , µN OB are Maximum growth rates of Nitromonas and Nitrobacter, respectively. This model is used for simulating the biological processes that take place in the platform proposed above, in order to predict the growth of bacteria and the resulting consumption of carbon and nitrogen. This is done in taking into account nitrite as an intermediate state variable during the nitrification and denitrification processes. [SOURCE: Page_101]

3.3.3   Implementation of WRRF platform Set-up of the ‘simulation platform’ was made under Matlab/Simulink-environment. By using three bioreactor objects, five 2-way flow combiner models, all implemented as C- code S-function objects. The membrane is supposed to be ideal (the effluent does not contain any particulate component). Furthermore, a hydraulic delay model was add (to avoid algebraic loops). and the controllers for internal recirculation flow rate recirculation flow rate with various connections and valves are integrated. [SOURCE: Page_101]

3.3.4   The Solids Retention Time (SRT) control The SRT is an important design and control parameter for the activated sludge process. It represents the average length of time that bacteria stay in the process before being wasted. The selected SRT directly affects the bacterial composition in an activated sludge system. [SOURCE: Page_101]

### 3.4. Platform simulation                                                                     85

In the literature, SRT is usually considered from a steady-state perspective. At steady-state, the static SRT is defined as the mass of microorganisms in the system divided by the mass of microorganisms wasted per time unit: [SOURCE: Page_102]

Xr Vr SRT =                                                   (3.3) Xw Qw + Qe Xe Where: Vr : Total volume of aeration tanks, m^3 ; Xr : Total suspended solids (T SS) concentration in the reactor, mg/L; Xw : Total suspended solids (T SS) concentration in the waste acti- vated sludge, mg/L; Xe : Total suspended solids (T SS) concentration in effluent,mg/L; Qw : Waste activated sludge (W AS) flow rate, m^3 /d; Qe: effluent flow rate, m^3 /d Xe = Xr in completely mixed reactor: [SOURCE: Page_102]

Vr SRT =                                            (3.4) Qw The first modeling step consists in determining the initial operating conditions of the WRRF associated with each operation mode: [SOURCE: Page_102]

3.3.4.1    Reuse Mode It is necessary to find conditions in which the biological treatment will make it possible to preserve the nitrogen under its form N H$_4$+ . It means that the sludge age (SRT) must not be too high, so that nitrification will be limiting or it will not take place. The tests are done with a sludge removal rate (SRR), Qw varying between 10 m^3 /d and 40 m^3 /d. It is assumed that no oxygen limitation occurs and no nitrite accumulates. [SOURCE: Page_102]

3.3.4.2    Complete treatment Mode The SRR applied in this mode must make nitrification possible to treat nitrogen. Simula- tions are made with Qw varying between 0.1 m^3 /d and 6 m^3 /d. [SOURCE: Page_102]

3.4       Platform simulation In order to analyze the platform and to determine the best transition strategy from one mode of operation to another in terms of quality and time response, it is necessary to identify the scenarios for each mode of operation. Each scenario represents a mode of operation. To study the transition from one operating mode to another, we study the switch of environ- mental conditions (positions of valves, values of control signals). Different switches are simulated and the corresponding time responses - corresponding to the time required for the system to switch from one steady state to another one - are observed. It should be noted that the valves in the different configurations are supposed to be all or nothing valves. Each value thus represents a direction of the corresponding fluxes. The first two scenarios are used to model the adaptive treatment (useful for reuse), while the second two scenarios represent the full - or complete - treatment (conventional treatment). [SOURCE: Page_102]

86        Chapter 3. Flexibility of Waste Resource Recovery Facilities for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_103]

Table 3.2: valve positions for each scenario [SOURCE: Page_103]

Valve A     Valve B    Valve C    Operating Mode 1           1          0        Scenario S$_1$ 1           0          0        Scenario S$_2$ 0           ∀          0        Scenario S$_3$ 0           ∀          1        Scenario S$_4$ [SOURCE: Page_103]

Remark 3.4.1. The symbol ∀ in Table 3.2 is used to designate the fact that the correspond- ing valve position has no importance: for instance if Valve A equals 0, the position of Valve B does not change anything to the configuration of the platform. [SOURCE: Page_103]

3.4.1     Scenario S$_1$ The anoxic and aerobic tanks (4 and 5) are not in operation, whereas the tank 6 is in operation, with the valves positioned at valve A = 1, valve B = 1. The internal and external recirculations are not activated Qext =Qint =0 and sludge extraction from the tank 6 is Qw = 32.4 L/d. [SOURCE: Page_103]

3.4.2     Scenario S$_2$ The anoxic tank (4) is not in operation, were the aerobic and MBR tanks (5 and 6) are in operation and aerated, the valves position are: valve A = 1, valve B = 0, valve C = 0. The external recirculation is activated and Qext = 270 L/d while the internal recirculation is not in activated Qint = 0 and sludge extraction from the MBR tank (6) Qw = 32.4 L/d. [SOURCE: Page_103]

3.4.3     Scenario S$_3$ During this scenario all tanks (4, 5 and 6) are in operation, the valve A = 0, B = ∀ and C = 0, the flows of internal and external recirculation are active and are directed towards the anoxic basin and sludge extraction from the 6 tank Qw = 1.4 L/d. [SOURCE: Page_103]

3.4.3.1    Scenario S4 In this scenario, the configuration is the same for the scenario S3, except for the valve C, that results in position 1. It means that the returned sludge is oriented to the aerobic tank. As we will see, the transitions dynamics between the different modes of operation depend a lot on the initial conditions. Usually, water for reuse is needed during spring and summer times while complete treatment is required during fall and winter where water should be treated to comply with discharge of water in the environment. To simulate such conditions, we varied the time at which we switch from one operation mode to another one. During the various simulations carried out, different components of the effluent are fol- lowed, in particular the different forms of nitrogen, as well as the bacterial populations present in the bioreactor. [SOURCE: Page_103]

### 3.5. Results and discussion                                                               87

Figure 3.2: Influence of retention time. Dynamic ammonium (blue), nitrate (green) and nitrite (red) concentrations for transitions at 100 days (continuous line) and at 200 days (dashed line) [SOURCE: Page_104]

3.5     Results and discussion [SOURCE: Page_104]

In the present work the results of simulation will be shown, focusing on the transition from the reuse mode to the full one or vice-versa, and describing the nitrogen dynamic during both modes. The alteration from the reuse to full mode had as goal the reduction of response time and to obtain N O$_3$− in effluent. On the dissimilarity,when the alteration from the full to reuse one, the objective was to reach a concentration of N H$_4$+ less than 30 mg.N/L. [SOURCE: Page_104]

3.5.1   Influence of retention time on reuse mode [SOURCE: Page_104]

Figure 3.2 shows the transition from the scenario S1 (reuse mode) to the scenario S3 (com- plete treatment mode). Different initial conditions at the time of switching explain the observed difference in time responses: remember that the XN OB bacteria grow exponen- tially. At 200 days the transition occurs. At this instant the XN OB biomass concentration is lower than the one when the transition occurs at 100 day (the more the bacteria are subjected to higher wasted sludge flow Qw = 32.4 L/d for prolonged time, the more its concentration are diminished). It should be noted that in practice, such difference may not be observed: depending on the time during which XAOB and XN OB have not been grown under appropriate conditions, they can simply be considered to be absent of the system. In other words, while simulations may consider very small concentration (that may have no physical meanings), XAOB and/or XN OB may have definitely been washed out from the system. We can also note that the duration of partial nitrification is longer in the case where the transition was applied at 200 day because of the growth rate of XAOB bacteria is greater than the growth rate of XN OB bacteria, then the growth of XAOB is faster to XN OB . To simulate realistic situations, such considerations should be studied with care. [SOURCE: Page_104]

88      Chapter 3. Flexibility of Waste Resource Recovery Facilities for water Reuse [SOURCE: Page_105]

Figure 3.3: Influence of returned sludge direction. Dynamic ammonium (blue), nitrate (green) and nitrite (red) concentrations in the effluent. [SOURCE: Page_105]

3.5.2   Influence of returned sludge direction Figure 3.3 shows the comparison between two transition cases. The first one is the switch from scenario S$_1$ to scenario S$_3$ (dashed line) whilst the second one is the swith from scenario S$_1$ to scenario S$_4$ (continuous line). The first transition occurs 200 days slower than the second one. In the later one the returned sludge was directed to the aerobic tank at the instant of transition. This set-up promotes the nitrifying bacteria growth (XAOB , XN OB ), since it reduces the lag phase. On the other hand, in the first transition, the returned sludge was directed to the anoxic tank. In this case, the initial growth of nitrifying bacteria at the transition instant is not encouraged, It leads to an increase in the lag phase. [SOURCE: Page_105]

3.5.3   Effects of dilution on the returned sludge The transition from S$_1$ to S$_4$ (from the reuse mode to the full treatment mode) was also simulated in considering two different values of the returned sludge, that were 100 L/d and 270L/d. In Figure 3.4 the profiles corresponding to this different values of returned sludge were plotted. When the flow of returned sludge equals 100 L/d (dashed line), the time response was shorter than 270 L/d (continuous line). In the same simulations, an increase in total nitrogen concentrations (N H$_4$+ , N O$_3$− and N O$_2$− ) was observed since low returned sludge flow means low dilution rate and more concentrated nutrients. So, in terms of time response the returned sludge equal to 100 L/d seems to be the most important but not in terms in terms of nutrient concentrations in the effluent. In addition, N O$_2$− is important during partial nitrification, which is problematic given the toxic characteristics of nitrite. [SOURCE: Page_105]

3.5.4   Influence of biomass addition on the reduction of response time (bio- augmentation) The next step is to focus on the use of bio-augmentation (addition of nitrifying bacteria) to minimize the time to switch from scenarios S1 to S4. From a simulation viewpoint, it [SOURCE: Page_105]

is equivalent to change initial condition of nitrifying bacteria (XAOB and XN OB ) in the reactors according to the following rules: [SOURCE: Page_106]

• XAOB = 10 mg/L; XN OB = 10 mg/L (dashed line in fig 3.5). [SOURCE: Page_106]

• XAOB = 1 mg/L; XN OB = 1 mg/L (continuous line in fig 3.5). [SOURCE: Page_106]

In figure 3.5, both profiles are represented when the added concentration of bacterial is 1 mg/L on both (XAOB and XN OB ). A short partial nitrification was observed because of an insufficient concentration of XN OB to obtain a sufficient growth and a full nitrification (N O$_2$− to N O$_3$− ). On the contrary, with the addition of 10 mg/L, a complete nitrification was observed. The addition of a more important bacterial concentration influenced posi- tively the time response, reducing it from 50 days with the addition of 1 mg/L to 30 days with 10 mg/L. The Bio-augmentation is therefore a solution in order to decrease the time response of the system as well as the consequent formation of nitrites. [SOURCE: Page_106]

3.5.5   Two cycles simulation of transitions from Reuse to Full treatment The figure 3.6 shows two transition cycles from the reuse mode to the complete mode. The first cycle was performed after 100 days of operation to the reuse mode to switch after that to full one again. The second cycle was started after 200 days in this cycle. The transition from the full to the reuse mode (from scenario S3 to S1) requires a limiting nitrification to obtain some N H4+ in the effluent. This transition is fast since it depends only on the time necessary for the nitrifying bacteria to be washed out using the waste sludge flow. This transition is completed in only 15 days (figure 3.6). When comparing the observed time responses for all simulated transitions, the addition of biomass (XAOB and XN OB ) at the time of transition seems to be the most appropriate [SOURCE: Page_106]

strategy. Indeed, autotrophic bacteria and in particular XN OB autotrophs develops very slowly. In addition, when working without bio-augmentation, the risk for nitrite accumu- lation in the transient is very high. [SOURCE: Page_107]

### 3.6. Conclusion                                                                         91

3.6    Conclusion A new flexible wastewater treatment configuration was proposed in this paper. The basic objective underlying its design is to improve the transition operation between the resource recovery mode (Reuse) to the conventional treatment mode (full). In the present study various possible scenarios were discussed with the aim to analyze and design different transition situations. Suitable scenarios were selected for each operating mode in order to determine the best transition to minimize the time response and to ensure effluent quality in accordance with agriculture requirements. An improvement on the response time by an addition of nitrifying bacteria was observed. A future work will investigate extensions of the results to take in account a dynamics influent flow with manipulating in different control degree. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Chapter 4. Processing Soil Water Content Measurements to Estimate Crop Water 94                                  Requirements and Optimize Irrigation Supply [SOURCE: Page_111]

This chapter is published : F. Karam, A. Mouneimne, F. Aichouche, A. Rapaport, and J. Harmand, “Processing soil water content measurements to estimate crop water require- ments and optimize irrigation supply,” in IFAC-PapersOnLine, 2019, vol. 52, no. 23. DOI : 10.1016/j.ifacol.2019.11.008 When considering a smart resources recovery, the flexible treatment system must be able to provide the treated wastewater according to the plant/crops requirements in terms of quality (notably nitrogen) and quantity (water irrigation). The best irrigation strategy for crops is to maintain the level of soil moisture in the root zone that exactly meets the needs of the plant/crop. In order to do so, the soil water content in the top soil profile must be observed. First this chapter provides a new preprocessing procedure to transform the raw measured signals into a signal that can be used within a water balance model. Secondly, we design an unknown input observer to estimate the part of water used by the plant for its evapotranspiration. The approach is validated using real time series data obtained from the monitoring of avocado culture under Lebanon’s Mediterranean climate. [SOURCE: Page_111]
