### 4.1. Abstract                                                                             95

4.1    Abstract In this paper, we present a new procedure using an observer for estimating soil water content (SWC), a key parameter of soils when dealing with the adaptation of water plant requirements within the framework of irrigation. Results showed that the estimation of SWC by means of the observer was good. On the one hand, the estimation of the unknown part of the soil-plant water balance equation (negative component) has been found to be noise-free and values of evapotranspiration ET, drainage Dr and runoff Ro were estimated after filtering the simulated data. On the other hand, SWC has been found to increase after each water supply and decrease in the interval separating two successive irrigations. In addition, crop water requirements of avocado were found to be as much as 622 mm (for the 2016 dataset) and 380 mm (for the 2018 dataset) during the growing season from June throughout September. The findings of this research helped accounting for the net water re- quirements of avocado with the aim to optimize irrigation supply and provide guidelines to growers and farmers on sustainable irrigation of this crop under Lebanon’s Mediterranean climate and limited water resources. [SOURCE: Page_112]

4.2    Introduction Avocado (Persea americana Mill) is a subtropical tree native to Central America not very demanding in terms of light and water and can withstand reduced water supplies [Nakasone et al. 1998], [Bower 1985], [Cutting et al. 1986] and [Bower & Jackson 2003]. However, yields, fruit quality and tree health will suffer from unbalanced water man- agement [Celedón et al. 2012]. When placed under water stress, avocado trees develop blockages, which are not reversible [Martens et al. 1994]. Therefore, significant, sustained stress periods will result in lasting damage to avocado’s tree capacity to draw water and nutrients [Dirou 2003]. With adequate water supplies, the trees will produce new shoots and fruits and the effect of the stress damage will diminish [Partridge 1997], [Carr 2013b]. [SOURCE: Page_112]

The standard irrigation strategy for crops is to maintain a certain level of soil moisture in the top root zone within the Readily Available Water (RAW) range for water sensitive crops (-20 kPa maximum) throughout the growing period [Carr 2013a], [Müller et al. 2016]. As avocado is an evergreen tree, the soil moisture should be monitored throughout the year and during the non-bearing period of the growth cycle. Irrigation is needed in summer and in some cases in spring whenever winter rainfall is not sufficient [Pegg 1978]. In all cases, good irrigation management practices combined with good soil management result in increased productivity of avocado trees [Karam et al. 2014]. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Under Mediterranean climate, characterized by hot and dry summers and dominantly [SOURCE: Page_112]

Chapter 4. Processing Soil Water Content Measurements to Estimate Crop Water 96                                  Requirements and Optimize Irrigation Supply [SOURCE: Page_113]

humid winters, irrigation is practiced to restore water deficits due to high crop evapotran- spiration (ET). In order to maintain adequate soil water content in the top soil profile, irrigation scheduling based on regular readings of the soil water content in the root zone has the advantage to supply the needed water requirements to crops to avoid prolonged periods of water stress. Avocado is known to have shallow root system expanding over the top 60 cm of the soil profile. Short irrigation intervals combined with reduced water applications have been shown to improve the tree performance and increase yield, while saving amounts of water that can be used to irrigate other crops [Karam et al. 2014]. [SOURCE: Page_113]

A first objective of this study was to monitor at online basis soil water content (SWC) in the root zone by means of digital tensiometers and determine crop water requirements of avocado under Lebanon’s Mediterranean climate. The second objective was to provide growers and farmers with practical information on sound irrigation management of this crop under the prevailing water shortages during summer period. [SOURCE: Page_113]

4.3     Soil water content measurement and dynamical modeling 4.3.1   Soil Water Content Measurement Soil water potential (in cbar) in the 0–45 cm of the soil profile is currently measured with resistive devices. Such sensors respond to changes in soil water potential at the desired depth. This type of digital tensiometers is an electrical conductor and thereby provides relative indication of the soil moisture status. As the soil dries up, water is removed from the sensor and the resistance measurement increases. Conversely, when the soil is rewetted, the resistance lowers. This is a non-destructive method that provides accurate indication on the soil water availability to plants without any maintenance and can remain in the soil during long periods of time. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Using such sensors, values of soil water content (SWC, in cbar) can be measured online and converted into mm of water depth using the following simple conversion equation [Karam et al. 2014]: [SOURCE: Page_113]

( SW C(t)| 100 cbar ) × F C(%υ) × 10 SW C|mm =                                                         (4.1) 15 [SOURCE: Page_113]

Where 100 is a factor to convert from cbar to bar and 15 is the pressure (in bar) at permanent wilting point, while FC is field capacity (in % of volume). 10 is a conversion factor to convert water content volumetric water (in %) into mm of water depth (1 % of volumetric water corresponds to 1 cm or 10 mm of water depth). [SOURCE: Page_113]

### 4.3. Soil water content measurement and dynamical modeling                               97

4.3.2   Water mass-balance modelling The water balance approach to irrigation scheduling keeps track of the soil water deficit by accounting for all water additions and subtractions from the soil root zone. Crop water consumption or evapotranspiration accounts for the biggest subtraction of water from the root zone while precipitation and irrigation provide the major additions [Andales 2014]. The different components of the soil–plant water balance can be used to write down the following water balance in soil over Q given period of time: [SOURCE: Page_114]

P + I − ET − Dr − Ro ± ∆Q = 0                              (4.2) [SOURCE: Page_114]

where P is precipitation, I irrigation, ET evapotranspiration, Dr drainage, Ro runoff, and ∆Q the variation of soil water content during a given irrigation interval. The mass balance 4.2 can be rewritten as the following continuous time ordinary dif- ferential equation : [SOURCE: Page_114]

dSW C(t) = f (SW C(t))(P (t) + I(t) − ET (t) − Dr (t) − Ro (t))            (4.3) dt where SWC is the Soil Water Content while f (SW C(t)) = 0 if SW C(t) < 0 and f (SW C(t)) = 1 otherwise to guarantee the positivity of SWC. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Remark 4.3.1. In this paper, we work with trees. Thus it is assumed that the volume of soil containing accessible water – the root zone - is constant. If we were considering developing crops, we would take into account its growing volume as proposed, for instance, in the model proposed in [Kalboussi et al. 2019]. [SOURCE: Page_114]

The dynamic of SW C depends on two kinds of terms, that we will denote here as ‘inputs’ because they act on its dynamic. Some of them are known (P and I notably) while others are unknown (as ET , Dr and Ro ). Our objective is to estimate what contributes to the negative influence of SW C in order to compensate it with irrigation. In other terms, we aim at providing a systematic procedure to continuously estimate the sum −ET −Dr −Ro. To do so, we know the dynamic of the mass balance of water content in soil, i.e. 4.3, and the in-real-time (or online) measurement of SW C(t) provided by 4.2. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Remark 4.3.2. In practice, SW C is measured at a given depth. In all rigor, we should write a model including the depth at which the sensor is positioned and write down the dynamic of SWC at this depth. However, this model would be much more complicated than 4.3. As a first approximation, we rather use 4.3 as a basis for estimating the unknown component of the dynamic of SW C as it is described in the next section. It is equivalent to assuming that water mass balance in soil is equivalent to a simple tank that fills up and empties depending on what is added (u) and withdrawn (w), respectively. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Chapter 4. Processing Soil Water Content Measurements to Estimate Crop Water 98                                  Requirements and Optimize Irrigation Supply [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.4    Observer design for estimating crop water requirements In the following, we use systems theory to build an observer for the term −ET − Dr − Ro . From now on, we denote u(t) = P (t) + I(t) and w(t) = −ET (t) − Dr (t) − Ro (t). In a systemic approach, u(t) is called a ‘known input’ while w(t) is qualified as an ‘unknown input’. Equation 4.3 is then rewritten as: [SOURCE: Page_115]

dSW C(t) = f (SW C(t))(u(t) + w(t))                         (4.4) dt In a first attempt, it was assumed that w(t) is an unknown constant (in practice it may vary slowly with time). Under this assumption, we can write the dynamic of w as: [SOURCE: Page_115]

dw =0                                         (4.5) dt Following the disturbance accommodation control theory (cf.          for instance [Harmand et al. 2000]), we consider the ‘augmented’ dynamical system under a ma- trix form:          "           #        !"       #      ! dSW C(t) dt 0  1   SW C(t)     1 dw       =                 +      u(t)              (4.6) dt 0 0     w(t)       0 which is simply a way of rewriting both 4.4 and 4.4 which are two simple first order dif- ferential ordinary equations in a concatenated manner as a single dynamical system of dimension 2. In addition, recall that SW C(t) is measured. Let us denote y(t) = SW C(t). Referring to control systems theory, we can then build an ‘observer’ (or an ‘estimator’) for w(t) - denoted ŵ(t)- computed as the ‘output’ of the following observer: " dξ (t) #           !"      #     !               ! 1 dt          0 1      ξ1 (t)   1        K$_1$ dξ2 (t)     =                 +   u(t) +    (y(t) − ŷ(t))                 (4.7) dt 0 0      ξ2 (t)   0        K$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_115]

Which rewrites: " dξ (t) #              !"      #      !              ! 1 dt          −K$_1$ 1       ξ1 (t)   1        K$_1$ dξ2 (t)     =                    +   u(t) +    y(t)                     (4.8) dt −K$_2$ 0       ξ2 (t)   0        K$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_115]

where ŷ(t) = ξ1 (t) and ŵ(t) = ξ2 (t) and K T = (K$_1$ K$_2$ )T is the ‘gain’ of the observer, chosen such that the system 4.8 guaranties that limt→inf (y(t) − ŷ(t)) = 0 [SOURCE: Page_115]

This modeling for w(t) resents an important drawback: since it assumes w varies slowly with time (rigorously, it is a constant!), it will give bad results if it is not the case. In order to relax this hypothesis, we decided to “augment” the model chosen for w in [SOURCE: Page_115]

### 4.5. Practical application                                                                  99

assuming it is the output of a second order system. Such an hypothesis can be formalized in replacing the model 4.4 by the following differential system: " dw (t) #             !"           # 1 dt         0 1         w^1 (t) dw^2 (t)    =                                           (4.9) dt 0 0         w^2 (t) with w(t) = w^1 (t) In other terms, w(t) = α + βt is now the sum of an unknown constant α, and a line which the slope β must be estimated. In doing so, 4.6 is rewritten as:           dSW C(t)                                         dwdt(t)   0       1 0   SW C(t)      1 1      = 0       0 1  w^1 (t)  + 0 u(t)                  (4.10)                                           dt     dw^2 (t)      0       0 0    w^2 (t)      0 dt [SOURCE: Page_116]

Finally the observer equation can be deduced for the new unknown input model:           dξ1 (t)                                                  dξdt(t)   −K$_1$      1 0   ξ1 (t)     1          K$_1$  2  = −K$_2$           0 1 ξ2 (t) + 0 u(t) + K$_2$  y(t)                     (4.11)  dt  dξ3 (t)     −K$_3$      0 0   ξ3 (t)     0          K$_3$ dt [SOURCE: Page_116]

where ŷ(t) = ξ1 (t) and ŵ(t) = ξ2 (t) and K T = (K$_1$ K$_2$ K$_3$ ). While system 4.4 is a system with one known and one unknown input, system 4.8 - or 4.11 - is a system – of higher dimension – but with two known inputs (u(t) and y(t)). In other words, replacing a system of one dimension with an unknown input with a higher dimensional system with only known inputs allows us to estimate the unknown input of the latter. In addition, playing with K we can choose the ‘speed’ (or the ‘rate’) at which the estimate converges towards the ‘true’ value (the actual value) of the unknown input. Of course, to guarantee that property, the system must be observable what can be easily checked for the above system whatever the model chosen for w. In practice, the observer 4.11 an then be used to estimate w(t) from the knowledge of a time series of u(t) and of y(t). In practice, it is well known that under semi-arid or arid climates, ET is the main contributor to w. As a consequence,estimating w allows us to estimate the crop requirements ET . [SOURCE: Page_116]

4.5     Practical application 4.5.1   The experimental setup Digital tensiometers (Watermark, Irrometer Inc., California) in the range of 0-199 cbar were installed at different depths in the 0-45 cm layer of the soil profile during two seasons: i) the 2016 growing season (June through September) and ii) the 2018 growing season, in two orchards cropped with avocado (Persea americana Mill) in Lebanon. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Chapter 4. Processing Soil Water Content Measurements to Estimate Crop Water 100                                 Requirements and Optimize Irrigation Supply [SOURCE: Page_117]

4.5.2    Observer design and results The proposed approach was applied to data obtained on the orchards described here above (cf. Fig. 4.1 to Fig. 4.6). The purpose was to provide online soil water monitoring in the root zone and determine crop water requirements using a proposed soil-plant water balance approach. The ultimate goal was to provide irrigation advisory on this crop to growers and farmers to better manage the limited available water resources. The sensor’s readings were converted from cbar in mm using Eq.4.1 before being in- jected in the observer Eq.4.11 . The observer gain was chosen using a try and error ap- proach using the 2016 dataset and applied without additional settings to the 2018 dataset. It is to be noticed that the available dataset is not noisy (cf. Fig. 4.2 to Fig. 4.5). Thus, it is expected the observer to be not very sensitive to noise and we can choose gains relatively high. In order to tune the observer, we decided to use an original optimization technique consisting in tuning K in such a way that the innovation – using one dataset – is minimized. It was checked a posteriori that the obtained observer was stable. Stabilizing gains used to obtain the results presented below were [184001800]T corresponding to closed loop poles of the observer - the system 4.11 arbitrarily placed at : [−6.11 − 5.95 + 17i − 5.95 − 17i]T . [SOURCE: Page_117]

Figure 4.1: Irrigation I(t), in mm over time (in days) – 2016 datase [SOURCE: Page_117]

First, whatever the dataset considered, it is to be noticed that because measured data are not noisy at all, choosing high gains guarantees a very fast convergence of the estimator towards the measured data of SW C (cf. Fig. 4.2). When analyzing Fig. 4.3 and Fig. 4.6, it is clear that the reconstructed signal ŵ(t) is indeed made of piecewise constants and abrupt [SOURCE: Page_117]

### 4.5. Practical application                                                          101

Figure 4.3: Estimated evapotranspiration ET , in mm over time – 2016 dataset [SOURCE: Page_118]

Chapter 4. Processing Soil Water Content Measurements to Estimate Crop Water 102                                 Requirements and Optimize Irrigation Supply [SOURCE: Page_119]

Figure 4.4: Irrigation I(t), in mm over time (in days) – 2018 dataset [SOURCE: Page_119]

slopes modeling the response of the plant to irrigation periods. In other words, after each irrigation event, the response of the plant – view as a constant plus a straight line has to be re-calibrated as proposed with respect to model 4.9. Second, notice that the estimated signal ŵ(t) is not only negative – while it is actually supposed to give an estimation of the sum of all negative contributions to the dynamic of SW C. There are several possible explanations. Either measurements or the input signal are a little bit biased. Having chosen high gains for the observer, there are some overshoots including negative estimations of SW C while it should be constrained to be positive. Globally however, the results are good and very interesting from a practical viewpoint: using an observer for estimating ET is very innovative and useful for users. [SOURCE: Page_119]

4.6    Discussion and conclusions Results obtained within the frame of this study showed that soil sensing was quantitative and accurate method, since it is based on online readings of the soil water potential at different depths of the soil profile, and convert the readings into water depth (in mm) that are used by the soil- plant balance through the proposed equation. [SOURCE: Page_119]

### 4.6. Discussion and conclusions                                                     103

Figure 4.5: Measured (in red) and estimated (in black) SW C, in mm over time – 2018 dataset [SOURCE: Page_120]

Figure 4.6: Estimated evapotranspiration ET , in mm over time – 2018 dataset [SOURCE: Page_120]

Chapter 4. Processing Soil Water Content Measurements to Estimate Crop Water 104                                 Requirements and Optimize Irrigation Supply [SOURCE: Page_121]

Results also showed that the proposed approach was good in simulating the dy- namic of soil water content (SW C) in the root zone as the sum of two components of the soil-plant water balance; the first negative with unknown outputs (ET , Dr and Ro ) and the other positive with known inputs (P and I). The goodness of the proposed approach depends on the availability of input variables and the estimation of unknown outputs. In other words, the proposed approach behaved as a ‘watcher’ of the different components of the soil-plant water balance system. It was demonstrated that when SW C is monitored at a regular basis, it becomes feasible and reliable calculating ET , being the major unknown output in this complex system. [SOURCE: Page_121]

On the other hand, the calculations of ET made with the proposed ‘watcher’ ap- proach accounted for the net irrigation requirements of avocado during the growing season from June throughout September. Net irrigation requirements of avocado (the integral of ŵ(t))) were found to as much as 622 mm for the 2016 dataset while it was 380 for the 2018 dataset (to be compared to the volumes of water that were brought and that were 532 mm and 324 mm respectively for both years). This finding helps optimizing irrigation supply by providing guidelines to growers and farmers on sustainable water management for this crop under Lebanon’s Mediterranean climate and water resources limitations. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Further validation of the proposed approach is still needed on other crops and dif- ferent environments. For future works, we suggest calculating net irrigation requirements assuming a threshold level of SW C as a fraction of the total available water in the soil at a desired depth. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Chapter 5. Modeling and optimal control for production-regeneration systems - 106                                                          preliminary results - [SOURCE: Page_123]

This chapter is published : F. Aichouche, N. Kalboussi, A. Rapaport and J. Har- mand, "Modeling and optimal control for production-regeneration systems - preliminary results -," 2020 European Control Conference (ECC), Saint Petersburg, Russia, 2020, DOI: 10.23919/ECC51009.2020.9143741. The quality requirements for reusing treated wastewater imposes the treatment to comply with strict normative constraints. In most cases, this has as a consequence to increase the treatment cost and discourage water reuse. As already mentioned, MBRs are good can- didates for treating wastewater to be reused since they allow the production of very high quality waters. However, they suffer of a very important drawback which is the necessity to manage membrane fouling. To decrease their operating costs, their functioning must be optimized. We developed an automatic tools for the optimization of membrane fouling management. More precisely, we propose an algorithm for minimizing the energy con- sumption under performance constraints using a generic modeling approach. The main characteristic of our results is its genericity since the hypotheses under which the results remain valid include a very large class of systems called ‘production’ and ‘regeneration’ systems. In this framework, a simple general model is introduced and the results of a closed-loop optimal control is synthesized. [SOURCE: Page_123]

### 5.1. Abstract                                                                            107

5.1    Abstract This work presents a general model for production - regeneration systems, alternating two functioning modes, the first one as a production process while the second one regenerates the process performances. Such systems are quite common in industry: they include for in- stance filtration/backwash of membranes, discharge/charge of accumulators. Basically, in these systems the sequence of production/regeneration over time cycles is the manipulating variable. A simple mathematical model involving a single explanatory ‘hidden variable’ to capture the dynamic behavior of the process in both functioning modes is designed. Based on the Pontryagin’s Maximum Principle, we characterize the optimal control strategy for a given criterion minimizing the energy consumption under a constraint on the performances. [SOURCE: Page_124]

5.2    Introduction Many systems in industry require alternating ‘production’ and ‘regeneration’, or ‘recov- ering’ and ‘maintenance’ modes. It is for example the case of membrane or accumula- tor systems. During the membrane filtration, the matter is accumulated until a occurring fouling phenomenon. After that, since it is necessary to remove this matter, a regenera- tion process of the membrane called ‘backwash phase’ is used [Sutherland & Chase 2011] [Robles et al. 2018]. Practically, the mass of matter accumulated onto the membrane sur- face can be measured online, and this variable could be chosen to describe the state of the membrane process in models [Lan et al. 2017]. In discharge/charge systems, the available energy depends on the quantity of energy stored either electro-chemically, hydraulically (or electro-statically). When the available energy goes down a given threshold, the system has to be recharged. In this case, the variable that best describes the state of the accumulator is the quantity of energy stored (typically a force or an available voltage). In both cases, the state of the system can be described by a single variable that changes monotonously with time: the mass of matter attached onto the membrane or the force (e.g. the volt- age) continuously increase during the production phase. Then, during the regeneration or maintenance phase - which also consumes energy and makes the system unavailable for production - the state of the system monotonously decreases (the mass attached onto the membrane or the inverse of the available voltage or force). However, for certain systems such as electric ones, the two phases could be operated simultaneously, as a convex com- bination of the dynamics of each phase. We address the control problem which consists in fixing a given performance to be attained (for instance a quantity of fluid to be filtered or a quantity of energy to be used for producing a good), the control variable being the sequence of production/regeneration cycles over time. Notice that there exists necessarily a trade-off between the times spent in production in regeneration phases: the time spent in the production phase brings us closer to our production goal. However, greater the [SOURCE: Page_124]

Chapter 5. Modeling and optimal control for production-regeneration systems - 108                                                          preliminary results - [SOURCE: Page_125]

time spent in production lower the efficiency of the process, thus necessitating to switch to the regeneration phase at certain instants. Therefore we shall look for control strate- gies minimizing the total energy required to attain the target. Such problems have been solved but for maximizing the net water production of a membrane filtration system over a fixed time horizon [Kalboussi et al. 2018] or the optimal energy management strategies of charge/discharge accumulators [Robles et al. 2017]. In the present work, the generic mod- eling of such systems is introduced, and a different cost function where a minimization of energy consumption is considered, once the level of production is fixed. In the literature of production/regeneration systems, the total production with a final constraint - or with free end time was already considered [Kalboussi et al. 2018] [Robles et al. 2017]. The pa- per is organized as follows. First, model and general assumptions are presented leading to an optimal control problem. Then, we apply the Pontryagin’s Maximum Principle (PMP), and discuss about singular arc and switching curve for the control synthesis. Finally, a numerical application is presented and discussed before conclusions and perspectives are given. [SOURCE: Page_125]

5.3    Modeling and preliminaries Let x(t) be a variable describing the state of the process at time t - for instance the mass of the membrane deposit during the water filtration or the inverse of available voltage at both terminals of a battery. During the production phase, we can assume that x(t) is growing according to a dynamics ẋ = fp (x). During the regeneration phase, this dynamics is re- versed according to a dynamics ẋ = −fr (x). We consider a control u(t) that takes values 1 during production and −1 during regeneration (or in [−1, 1] if a combination of both modes is possible). Then, the controlled dynamics can be written as follows: 1+u          1−u ẋ =  fp (x) −     fr (x),                x(0) = x0 . (5.2) Let us assume that the total energy demand is the sum of the energy needed during the production phase and the one required for regeneration: it is given by a function ET that depends on x(t), such that: Z tf  1 + u(t)             1 − u(t)  ET (x0 , u(·)) =                     lp (x(t)) +          lr (x(t) dt    (5.3) 0          2                    2 [SOURCE: Page_125]

Remark 5.3.1. As one has Q+ > 0 and Q+ > Q− , the target can be reached in finite time, taking for instance the control u = 1 at any time. However, it is well known from the theory of optimal control that the existence of an optimal trajectory can be guaranteed when the velocity set is convex. Therefore, we shall consider for the mathematical analysis that the control u(·) can take values in the interval [−1, 1]. The question of practical applicability of a control that takes values different to −1 and 1 relies then on bang-bang approximations. [SOURCE: Page_127]

When a membrane operates in filtration, the flow resistance is never null and increases according to the mass x formed on the membrane surface, which subsequently decreases the permeate flux. Thus, we assume that the rate fp at which the mass of material adheres to the membrane surface during filtration is a positive decreasing function. When changing to backwash operation, the mass is decomposed and the membrane’s permeability increases again. Therefore, the mass detachment can be described by a positive increasing function fr . [SOURCE: Page_127]

Consider an initial condition with x^0 < x̄. The control u = −1 makes x(·) decreasing, remaining in {x < x̄} and then cannot switch to reach the target with u = +1, which contradicts its optimality (Lemma 5.4.1). We deduce that u = +1 is optimal at any x < x̄. If the initial condition belongs to D+ with x^0 ≥ x̄, none of the solutions with u = +1 or u = −1 reaches a locus of commutation S or C. Therefore, u = +1 is optimal until tf . So, u = +1 is optimal when the state belongs to D+ . Consider now an initial condition in D− . The optimal control has to switch. Otherwise, it will be equal to +1 and as x(·) with u = +1 is increasing, it will have to cross the switching curve C. So u = −1 is optimal until the trajectory reaches C (and then enters the sub-domain D− ) or reaches S. u = −1 is thus optimal when the state belongs to D− . Finally, when an optimal trajectory reaches S from below or from above, it has to stay on S with the control ū until it exits S. If not, it leaves S either with u = −1 and enters the sub-domain D+ (but we have proved that u = +1 is optimal on this domain), either with u = 1 and enters the sub-domain D− (and u = − is optimal on this domain). [SOURCE: Page_136]

### 5.9. Conclusion                                                                        121

5.9    Conclusion We have proposed a methodology to determine an optimal feedback synthesis. This synthesis looks similar to the one of filtration problems with fixed terminal time [Kalboussi et al. 2018], but the determination of the singular arc is here much more com- plex, requiring the consideration of the auxiliary functions ∆, Γ, λ̄p and γx̄ . For linear dynamics, this leads to explicit polynomials whose coefficients can be determined analyti- cally from the parameters of the problem. A future work will investigate extensions of the results to more general classes of dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_138]

### 5.9. Conclusion                                                                           123

Conclusions and perspectives At the present time, wastewater could be said to be a ‘load’ : rejecting it into the environment is a kind of ‘threat’. It is necessary to treat it after use to ensure a quality that respects discharge limits and avoid direct or indirect environmental problems. At an extreme limit, the water can be treated at a degree allowing its reuse as tap wa- ter...but at undesirable and unsustainable environmental and economical prices. Indeed, such a treatment requires the use of an important energy cost (notably for nutrient removal). [SOURCE: Page_140]

But wastewater is also to be considered as an opportunity, notably for resource re- covery. When regarded as a non conventional source for water and nutrients, it is a valuable alternative to the mobilization of natural water of good quality and wastewater treatment plants ‘WWTPs’ should turn out to water resource recovery facilities ‘WRRFs’. Recovery can include different - and more or less innovative - options such as energy (for instance through biogas production) and nutrients recovery (mainly nitrogen and phosphorus but also possibly other molecules of interest through ‘biorefinery’). [SOURCE: Page_140]

The work presented in this manuscript is devoted to draw a picture for the future and to give a perspective of the treatment and resource recovery for the reuse of wastewater in agriculture. To maximize the flexibility of WRRF, it is proposed to make intensive use of advanced modelling and control techniques and to develop methodologies and tools for improving the functioning of such processes. Such a flexible system can allow the user to manage several operating modes to ensure a supply of a quality adapted to the plant/crop requirements, without ignoring some situations where the reuse is not the suitable alternative. In such a case, the proposed approaches should be used to optimize the actual treatment plants. [SOURCE: Page_140]

Globally, the main achievements of this study are summarized hereafter. In contrast to the conventional approach of separating the sludge produced from the wastewater treated and transforming it before using it in agriculture as a ‘fertilizer’, the new approach adopted in this study is able to recover the water and its nutrients by adapting its quality and quantity according to the crops requirements in real time and realize ‘fertigation’. To do so and using advanced modeling and control industrial techniques, a new flexible platform is proposed that can operate in several configuration modes. The platform is used firstly within an open loop approach strategy making intensive use of simulations. Such an approach could be further exploited to apply artificial intelligence strategies (as neural [SOURCE: Page_140]

Chapter 5. Modeling and optimal control for production-regeneration systems - 124                                                          preliminary results - [SOURCE: Page_141]

networks) to provide relevant control laws. To be more generic, and depending on the objective pursued, two improved closed-loop approaches were developed and evaluated in the presence of different uncertainty and disturbances. These approaches were synthesized in coupling a crop model with closed-loop industrial approaches. More specifically, the crop model was used as a basis for solving optimal control problems, the solutions of which were used as setpoints for the WRRF. The approach was successfully applied to six possible configurations of the flexible platform both in the absence and in the presence of disturbances. In addition, the results were compared to the performances of a conventional treatment approach. The platform coupled with the crop requirement was capable of generating an optimal requirement in terms of nitrogen. The results indicated that there are configurations that can save more energy while providing less nutrients, and vice versa (other configurations were delivering more nutrients but with more energy consumption). The conventional approach was found to be the one that consumes the most energy while not valorizing the nutrients. [SOURCE: Page_141]

The importance of considering the case when the reuse mode is not always the best solution for wastewater management brought us to improve the WRRF plant to be able to switch to conventional treatment mode in a minimum time, as well as the reverse, when switching from a conventional treatment mode to a reuse mode if necessary. This issue has been studied in order to improve both the flexibility of the proposed platform and to illustrate, through simulated scenarios, the most relevant control handles for this type of problem. This approach was successful and the switching time from one mode to another was minimized using a bio-augmentation strategy. [SOURCE: Page_141]

To provide the good quantity and quality of water, crops water stress must be mon- itored. As a preliminary study to provide information on the water requirements for our treatment system on the one hand and to optimize irrigation by supplying the exact amount of water required by the crops on the other hand, a new processing procedure was developed. With the help of an observer, we have succeeded in estimating the part of water used by the plant for its evapotranspiration. This study is supposed to provide guidelines to growers and farmers on sustainable water management as well as to future reuse strategies even if practical validation is needed on different crops and environments. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Finally, to adopt the previously developed platform application as an alternative to WTTP and to use it as a WRRF, we decided to improve its flexibility: this has been done [SOURCE: Page_141]

### 5.9. Conclusion                                                                          125

in chapters 1 and 2 in upgrading its energy efficiency. Since it uses a membrane as a filtration system, and because it represents the most energy-consuming component, an automatic control strategy was developed that could operate the membrane in an optimal way to reduce energy consumption under performance constraints. As preliminary results, a generic model was used and the optimal feedback synthesis was performed. It should be further developed for a general membrane model in future works. [SOURCE: Page_142]

Furthermore, it is important to notice that an originality of the present study with respect to others that can be found in the literature is that it seems to be one of the first ones where we investigate the principle of adapting the quality of treated water to the crops requirements in coupling a model of a flexible platform with an irrigation model. Using large-scale modeling and dynamic systems tools, we developed a number of control algorithms capable of improving the functioning of the WRRF. [SOURCE: Page_142]

Perspectives might be divided into the following aspects. For improving the plat- form modeling, it should be noted that the flexibility is one of the main characteristics. Special efforts were made during the construction of the platform in order to ensure its applicability to other benchmarks or other case studies that might become relevant. Considering modelling and simulation, the flexibility is especially important as WWTP flowsheets are frequently changing to achieve, for instance, adapted required quality. One might also want to evaluate a different and innovative process by comparing it with a reference one. Also, the modeling of specific parts of the process is rapidly evolving nowadays, such as the qualification and quantification of nitrous oxide emissions, which represents an important pollutant factor. Other nutrients of interest such as phosphorus should also be included. The modelling platform could also be improved by integrating the dynamic of some micropollutant, such as emerging compounds and pathogens notably to generate data to be used in risk assessment approaches. Another important effort to be made when taking into account environmental impact assessment consists in the coupling of an LCA (Life cycle analysis) model with the platform in order to evaluate potential gains of using the platform and evaluate environmental impacts. [SOURCE: Page_142]

Although the platform did not present a limitation regarding the level of water qual- ity provided except in the case where there was not enough nitrogen in the influent and the amount required was higher than the amount in the influent, it would be interesting to study the problem of mixing several qualities of waters in order to provide a quality [SOURCE: Page_142]

Chapter 5. Modeling and optimal control for production-regeneration systems - 126                                                          preliminary results - [SOURCE: Page_143]

adapted to - possible new - usages and/or needs. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Another important effort to be made is the consideration of possible improvements in WRRF platform designIn this sense, efforts have to be made in order to consider the platform modelling in a rigorous way or at least to validate the uncertainties linked to a plant size range to allow the redesign of alternative configurations. Accordingly, one might think of an exhaustive list of perspective scenarios (with design changes) to be evaluated and optimized such as the inclusion of anaerobic ammonium oxidation in the mainstream as well as a multitude of processes for the treatment and reuse of wastewater. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Considering that the influents dynamics may have nutrient levels that vary over time (in some cases the amount of nutrient present in the influent may reach a level sufficient to meet the needs while in other periods this level will never be reached) it is important to look at the problem of producing a nutrient-rich quality when time allows it and to use a storage system that could use this quality when needed. It requires to model the dynamic of stored waters, notably with respect to pathogens, which is a difficult task. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Finally, in view of the optimization aspects, the methodology is evolving rapidly and recent advances in machine learning have been helpful in the area of cost optimization. According to the best of our knowledge, the use of these tools for efficient function analysis and approximation has not been used within the context of water treatment for reuse: this could represent an important gain in terms of cost optimization of the WRRF platform, but also when the optimization is subject to multiple stiff constraints. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Appendices [SOURCE: Page_144]

## A PPENDIX B

Guideline Matlab/Simulink WRRF platform Implementation [SOURCE: Page_150]

The WRRF platform was implemented according to the benchmark guidelines (Copp, 2002) in the Matlab/ Simulink, following the approach already used in [Marsili Libelli et al. 2001] for implementing the kinetics as compiled C-code,from which a Dynamic Linked Library (DLL) is produced by compiling the source C-code with the mex Matlab command. At run time the DLL is invoked by the pertinent S-function block in the Simulink scheme. This arrangement combines the advantages of both platforms (Matlab and C) making the execution speed of compiled C available in the Simulink graphic lan- guage. The software organization is shown in figure B.1. The Matlab launch file is the core file and it supervises the entire simulation: after loading the pertinent input file and the specified model parameters and stoichiometric matrix, it invokes the Simulink module which performs the simulation using the precompiled C-files in the S-function blocks. The usage of precompiled DLLs is the preferred method of introducing complex models in a Simulink diagram, where each S-function block points to a DLL which is invoked at run time. This hierarchical model organization is shown in Fig. B.2. The S-function block in the Simulink diagram invokes a DLL generated by prior compilation of a C source code, according to the Matlab API directives. The implementation of the full benchmark model is based on six custom S-function blocks, which are now briefly described and whose con- nection stubs are shown in Fig. B.3. [SOURCE: Page_150]

## A PPENDIX D

The optimal requirement for agriculture system [SOURCE: Page_161]

For the agricultural system, we consider a crop grown in monoculture that is irrigated continuously with treated wastewater, for example with a drip irrigation system. The aim here is to determine how to supply water and nitrogen to the crop through irrigation for optimal crop growth. Although we do not consider that the water flow-rate through the WWTP can be controlled, we will suppose that the irrigation flow-rate is a controllable input of the crop system. Thus, the aim here is to determine the nitrogen concentration of the irrigation water as well as the irrigation flow-rate to supply for optimal crop growth. [SOURCE: Page_161]

As a first approach, we use an idealized model that considers a simplified crop de- velopment process and focuses on the water and nitrogen dynamics of the crop and soil. This dynamical system by [Pelak et al. 2017] simulates the crop-soil system on a uniform area of land, at the scale of a field, over the course of a growing season. The crop is represented by the canopy cover (the fraction of ground covered by a crop) and plant biomass per unit area. The other model variables are the vertically averaged relative soil moisture and nitrogen content per unit area of soil. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Table D.1: Crop model: State and input variables. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Description [Units] C      Canopy cover [m^2 m−2 ] B      Crop biomass per unit area [kg m−2 ] S     Relative Soil moisture [m^3 m−3 ] N      Nitrogen content per unit area of soil [g m−2 ] I     Irrigation flow-rate per unit area [mm d−1 ] FN     Nitrogen concentration of irrigation water [g m−3 ] [SOURCE: Page_161]

The dynamics are given in [Pelak et al. 2017]. The only adaptation introduced in this [SOURCE: Page_161]

146                      Appendix D. The optimal requirement for agriculture system [SOURCE: Page_162]

work is to set the fertilization term F (t) = I(t)FN (t), the product of the irrigation flow rate per unit area and the nitrogen concentration of the irrigation water. The state-space model of the system is ẋC (t) = fC (xC (t), uC (t)|θC ) ,                     (D.1) T with state xC = C B S N ∈ R$_4$≥0 and input uC = I FN ∈ R$_2$≥0 . The time-invariant                                                       [SOURCE: Page_162]

dynamics fC (·|θC ) depend on a set of parameters [Pelak et al. 2017] denoted by θC . We consider the parameters referring to the dynamics of a modern corn cultivar grown on a silty loam type soil. As a simplification, we consider the absence of any rainfall and assume constant ref- erence evapotranspiration, the water loss due to the impact of climate on evaporation and plant transpiration. [SOURCE: Page_162]

Optimal control formulation To determine the irrigation and fertilization requirements for optimal crop growth, we con- sider the problem of maximizing crop biomass at the end of the growing season. We write this as an optimal control problem, over a fixed time horizon that represents a growing season. [SOURCE: Page_162]

max      B(T )                                            (D.2a) uC (·) [SOURCE: Page_162]

s.t.      ẋC (t) = fC (xC (t), uC (t)|θC ),               (D.2b) ∀t∈[0,T ] max uC (t) ∈ [0, I         ] × [0, FNmax ],          (D.2c) xC (0) = x^0 ,                                    (D.2d) [SOURCE: Page_162]

with a given fixed final time T , initial state x^0 , and input upper bounds I max for maximum irrigation and FNmax for maximum nitrogen. Restrict ourselves to growing seasons lasting T = 139d. [SOURCE: Page_162]

The optimal control problem is solved using a dynamic programming approach for maximising the crops biomass. [SOURCE: Page_162]

159 [SOURCE: Page_177]

Contribution of modelling and control approaches to improve the flexibility of treatment systems for REUSE of treated wastewater in agriculture [SOURCE: Page_177]

Abstract in English To meet the challenges of the scarcity of good quality water re- sources, the reuse of treated wastewater ("REUT" or "REUSE") appears to be a solution for the future. It prevents the treated water from being released into the environment with- out giving it a second life, or even more. To do this, we are promoting a paradigm shift in which wastewater is no longer seen as waste to be depolluted but a new resource that can be treated on a custom basis, depending on the needs and uses in new energy recovery facili- ties, to recover nutrients and other molecules of interest. This sustainable management of water resources comes up against many challenges, in particular technical problems such as the lack of flexibility of current wastewater treatment plants. Faced with the great chal- lenge of providing, in a dynamic and economically acceptable manner, a quality of treated water adapted to the needs of agriculture, and in order to reduce the environmental impacts, automatic control technologies certainly have an essential role to play. This study, which is freed from current regulatory limitations as it is essentially carried out in simulations, presents the preliminary results of research with the development of a simulation platform for a flexible system combining activated sludge treatment plants with a membrane filtra- tion process. We evaluate how the control of the whole, based on the use of industrial controllers and advanced control, can be used to adapt in real time, under conditions of martial measurements and in the presence of uncertainties and disturbances, a water whose the quality meets the needs of the plants / crops or, on the contrary, when necessary, meets the quality constraints required for release into the environment. The approach requires knowing the needs of plants. As the latter are difficult to continuously measure, part of the work is dedicated to the synthesis of unknown input observers to estimate the amount of water available for the plant in the soil (and therefore accessible for evapotranspiration). Finally, since the cost of wastewater treated by membrane filtration technologies is often prohibitive for users, we are evaluating the extent to which recourse to optimal control approaches, in particular the Maximum Pontryagin Principle, would make it possible to reduce these cost by optimizing the management of the clogging of these membranes and thus minimizing the functioning cost of these systems. [SOURCE: Page_177]

Keywords: Flexibility, Modelling, Control, Reuse, Optimization, Wastewater treatment , Observation. [SOURCE: Page_177]

160 [SOURCE: Page_178]

Contribution des approches de modélisation et de contrôle pour améliorer la flexibilité des systèmes de traitement pour la réutilisation des eaux usées traitées en agriculture [SOURCE: Page_178]

Résumé en Français Pour répondre aux enjeux de la raréfaction des ressources en eau de bonne qualité, la réutilisation des eaux usées traitées (‘REUT’ ou ‘REUSE’) ap- paraît comme une solution d’avenir. Elle permet de ne pas rejeter l’eau traitée dans l’environnement sans lui faire subir une seconde vie, voire davantage. Pour cela, nous pro- mouvons un changement de paradigme dans lequel les eaux usées ne sont plus vues comme des déchets à dépolluer mais une nouvelle ressource pouvant être traitée à façon, dépen- dant des besoins et des usages dans de nouvelles installations de récupération d’énergie, de nutriments et autres molécules d’intérêt. Cette gestion de la ressource en eau se heurte à de nombreux défis, notamment des problèmes techniques tels que le manque de flexibilité des stations d’épuration actuelles. Face au grand défi de fournir, de manière dynamique et économiquement acceptable, une qualité d’eau traitée adaptée aux besoins de l’agriculture, et afin de réduire les impacts environnementaux, les technologies de l’automatique ont assurément un rôle essentiel à jouer. Cette étude, qui s’affranchit des limitations règlemen- taires actuelles car essentiellement menée en simulations, présente les résultats prélimi- naires de recherches combinant le développement d’une plateforme de simulation d’un système flexible combinant des systèmes de traitement à boues activées à un procédé de filtration membranaire. Nous évaluons comment le contrôle de l’ensemble, fondé sur l’utilisation de controleurs industriels et de commande avancée, peut être utilisé pour adapter en temps réel, en conditions de mesures martielles et en présence d’incertitudes et de perturbations, une eau dont la qualité répond aux besoins des plantes/cultures ou, au contraire, lorsque nécessaire, est conforme aux exigences de qualité requises pour un rejet dans l’environnement. L’approche nécessite la connaissance des besoins des plantes. Ces dernières étant difficilement mesurables en continu, une partie du travail est dédié à la synthèse d’observateurs à entrées inconnues pour estimer la quantité d’eau disponible pour la plante dans les sols (et donc accessible pour l’évapotranspiration). Enfin, le coût de l’eau usées traitée par les technologies de filtration membranaire étant souvent rédhibitoire pour les irrigants, nous évaluons dans quelle mesure le recours à des approches de contrôle optimal, notamment le Principe du Maximum de Pontryagin, permettrait de faire baisser ces coût en optimisant la gestion du colmatage de ces membranes et dionc le coût de fonc- tionnement de ces systèmes. [SOURCE: Page_178]

Mots clés: Flexibilité, Modélisation, Contrôle, Réutilisation, Optimisation, Taitement des eaux usées, Observation. [SOURCE: Page_178]
